PREFACE 

It is two years since the author was invited by the Trustees of McIndoes Academy to write an historical sketch of the school for the 100th Anniversary of its founding. Having been closely associated with the institution as the son of a former Trustee and as a member of the Class of 1919, he felt honored by the invitation and under some obligation to accept, particularly since it was extended by a classmate and boyhood friend, now one of the Trustees. 

The first problem which presented itself was one of obtaining material, and it was serious, for only six or seven catalogues were available for the first half-century, and, prior to the 1890's, there were no printed programs to be had. Thanks to the presence at the St. Johnsbury Athenaeum of the complete set of the bound volumes of the Caledonian, the writer was able to obtain much material by consulting some three thousand issues of that paper, in addition to several hundred copies of the Vermont Union. Further valuable research of this sort was done by Bessie H. Little who has skilfully examined other hundreds of issues of the Caledonian. 

Another source of material and encouragement has been frequent correspondence and conversation with several of the older alumni principally Kate P. Bedell, Jean H. Burrill, D. Roger Crane, Mabel, Robert, and William J. Frazer, Milo A. Gibson, Alice P., and 

Horace W. Gleason, Harriet L. Kendall, Bessie H. Little, and

Alfred K. Moore. These friends have supplied many catalogues, pictures, and programs. Their help has been invaluable and is keenly appreciated. An acknowledgment of such items is made in the notes. Others who have assisted in compiling lists and in providing information and material are the following: Winnie J. Aiken,

Dorothy H. Bailey, Mary F. Blanchard, Gladys C. Bogie, Edward 

E. Bruce, Larkin H. Burrill, Burleigh N. Carpenter, Editor John D.

Chase, Elsie A. Choate, Lois E. Cole, L. Thornton Conant, Leola 

C. Craigie, Beatrice P. Darling, George E. Davies, Hazel W. Dean, Edith A. Emery, Dorothy and William D. Flanders, Lyle M. Frazer, George H. Gardner, Barbara, Florence, John and Sandy Gibson, Alexander H. Gilchrist, Beth B. Gilchrist, Charles L. Gleason, William A. Glenn, Edith B. Gookin, Osman P. Hatch, Roger W. Higgins, Herman E. Hoyt, Catherine H. Howard, Julia F. Hunt, Leslie 

M. Hunt, Lyman C. Hunt, Mrs. Jackman, Edward H. Kenerson, Carrie J. Leighton, Agnes H. Little, Nellie A. Manchester, Lillian 

B. McGill, Irwin L. Moore, Jeane P. Moore, Wesley L. Moore, M. 

Claude Moore, Chandler B. Mosher, Darwin M. Nichols, Merwyn 

E. Pearl, Jean D. Phelps, Leonard A. Phelps, Paul E. Pitkin, Norman A. Powell, Harold S. Rising, Grace B. Roy, Mary B. Slade, Editor H. Gordon Smith, Henry G. Smith, Howard W. Smith,

Edgar G. Spear, Harold D. Somers, Dorothy N., and Floyd E. Som erville, Wendell P. Stafford, Lewis J. Streeter, Jennie M. Sweet, A.

Lorette Thompson, Alice W. Van Dyke, Edith H. Wallace, Jose phine M. Watts, Violet G. White, and Flora M. Winch. To these friends and to all others who have rendered service, the author is grateful.

The author is under great obligation to the members of the

staff of the St. Johnsbury Athenaeum for their unfailing courtesy and substantial help. He wishes to express his appreciation to the staffs of the Baker Library, the Boston Public Library, the Cobleigh Library, the Barnet and Monroe Libraries, and the Oliver Wendell 

Holmes Library.

He is likewise indebted to the New Hampshire 

State Library, the New Hampshire Historical Society, the Vermont State Library, the staff of the Vermont Historical Society and particularly to Dr. Arthur W. Peach, Director, for his invaluable help in research, suggestions, and proofreading. 

The Alumni Offices at the University of Vermont, Johnson, Middlebury, Amherst, Dartmouth, Brown, Harvard, Plymouth, and other institutions have been of great assistance in the search for biographical information. The State Departments of Education in New Hampshire and Vermont have provided material. His warm thanks and appreciation go to his good friends James J. Ritchie and George L. Frazer, Town Clerks, in making available local records.

Throughout the two years of research and writing, the author has often been the guest of his life-long friends, Marian and Robert Kendall, who have provided cordial hospitality, much assistance in local research, and constant encouragement, so appreciated in a long and arduous task. 

He is also greatly indebted to his wife for her patience, timely suggestions, and priceless assistance in revising and typing the manuscript, without which help the History could never have been completed for the Centennial.

It has been his good fortune to have had the full cooperation and active interest of the Trustees, headed by Albert D. Gilmour, the Alumni Association, whose President is Larkin H. Burrill, and the members of the Centennial Committee, Albert D. Gilmour and Dorothy N. Somerville, Co-Chairmen, Lyle M. Frazer, Pro gram Chairman, Marian A. Kendall, Secretary-Treasurer, Robert

L. Kendall, Publicity, Agnes H. Little, Pageant, and Patricia L. 

McLure, Music.

It has been the aim of the writer to portray the Academy against the background of the village and the region, and as the focal point of secondary education and of cultural and social life for the people of the surrounding communities. In order to do so, he has employed the style of a local newspaper correspondent, chronicling interesting occurrences each year, and inserting timely references to events on larger scenes of action in order to orient the reader and provide historical perspective. There has been an attempt to avoid generalities and to present documented material, arranged so as to portray life in the past and to revive recollections of the old days. 

Unless otherwise indicated, newspaper references are from the Caledonian. There are undoubtedly errors of commission and omission for which the writer makes due apology. 

As the work has progressed, he has become increasingly appreciative of the courage, faith, and determination of the founders of the Academy and of their successors who, despite countless discouragements and obstacles, have loyally supported the institution with their donations of money, time, and work. To them, and to their faith and devotion, the author pays tribute.

CHAPTER I EARLY DAYS IN VERMONT 

"A state exists in its history. Take away the memory of the past and what remains? Take away the example and the records of the past and what ray of light would be left? If we are bound to respect the claims of posterity, we likewise owe a debt to our ancestors." 1 

An historical sketch of a school should examine the cultural, economic, and social factors influencing its founding and should also discuss its relationship to the educational movements of the day and to the people it serves. A school, such at McIndoes 

Academy, whose century of service we honor in 1953, is best studied in the life of the society which gave it birth and in the lives of the many hundreds of men and women who have nurtured it in its development and whom it has served so well during the past hundred years.

We shall need to consider briefly the history of the Upper Connecticut Valley, if we wish to understand the background and the life and service of McIndoes Academy, which received its charter from the State of Vermont December 1, 1853. 2 

One hundred years before its establishment, the British and the French were marshaling their armies for the bloody and decisive French and Indian War (1756-63). The region now called Vermont was then known as the New Hampshire Grants and was claimed by the disputing Royal Provinces of New Hampshire and New York. 

The first permanent settlement in the Green Mountain State was made at Brattleboro just a century before the founding of this school. 3.

Two hundred years ago this whole region was a heavily-forested wilderness crossed by well-worn Indian trails. It was used by the savages for fishing and hunting and was broken only by occasional meadows, such as those at Newbury and Haverhill, where the Indians planted their corn and pumpkins every spring. It will be recalled that French-Indian bands, returning to Canada with prisoners after raids on Old Deerfield and Haverhill, Mass. in 1704, came north to the Wells River and then crossed overland to Canada. In order to protect the settlers from similar attacks, a log fort was built that same year by Captain Wells just north of the spot where the stream bearing his name flows into the Connecticut.4 

It was some time, however, before settlers ventured into the upper reaches of the beautiful Connecticut Valley. although the region was seen by John Stark in 1752 on his march into Indian captivity in Canada. It was explored in 1754 by Captain Peter 

Powers who, traveling with a small party up the Pemigewasset and Baker rivers, followed the Indian portage trail past the Baker Ponds to the Connecticut. Proceeding to the Ammonoosuc, Powers then skirted the Gardner Range and pushed as far as Israel's River in Lancaster. 5

The strong blow against the St. Francis Indians in 1759 at their camp near Sorel, Quebec, an attack made by Major Robert Rogers

and his Rangers, did much to lessen the Indian menace. It is of local interest to note that survivors of Rogers' expedition passed down the Passumpsic and Connecticut Rivers on their painful retreat to Fort Number Four at Charlestown, N. H. The capture of Quebec that same year and the termination of the French and Indian War in 1763 made possible the opening of the region to widespread settlement. 6 130

CHAPTER II SETTLEMENT OF BARNET 

Following the surrender of Montreal in 1760, some American colonial officers, returning homeward to Massachusetts and southern New Hampshire, passed down the upper Connecticut Valley and, liking the region, decided to settle in Newbury and Haverhill. 

These towns were chartered by Governor Benning Wentworth of New Hampshire May 18, 1763, the first settlers having arrived the previous year. Among them was Colonel Hazen, brother of the builder of the Hazen Road, the military highway constructed in 1776-79 from Wells River across the rugged hills of Caledonia 

County then part of Gloucester County towards Canada. ¹

Several months later, September 16, 1763, Barnet was chartered by Governor Wentworth, Ryegate having received its charter eight days earlier. The neighboring towns of Lyman, N. H. and Peacham were granted charters in 1761 and 1763, respectively. Several of the original proprietors of Barnet were members of the Stevens family, their descendants being long prominent in Vermont and in 

London. 2 

1 The first settlement in Barnet was made in 1770 when Elijah, Daniel, and Jacob Hall, and Jonathan Fowler established themselves near the falls of the Stevens River, where a sawmill and a gristmill were erected under the direction of a Colonel Hurd. In 

1774 General Jacob Bayley and Colonel Samuel Stevens measured the distance between the sites of McIndoe Falls on the Connecticut and Swanton Falls on the Missisquoi. 3 

The main settlement in Barnet was made in 1775 when Alexander Harvey, agent for a Scotch company, the United Company of Farmers, brought a group of Scotch settlers to occupy land purchased from Samuel Stevens in New York the previous year. This settlement paralleled that made in Ryegate in 1773-74 by James Whitelaw, agent for the Scots-American Company of Farmers. 

These two colonization projects were unique in Vermont, since they were based on immigration, not from southern New England, but from Scotland. 4

The Revolutionary War increased the hazards confronting these new settlements. The British-Indian raids on Royalton and Peacham in 1780, the activities of Committees of Safety and Correspondence in this part of Gloucester County, the formation of militia companies in Newbury, Bath, and Barnet, the construction of the Hazen Road in 1776-79, the frequent patrol of that highway and of the route joining the blockhouses in Corinth, Barnet, and Lanall these developments and measures indicate the perils facing the early settlers of this region. Despite these unsettled conditions, religious services were held during this period. 5 caster 

---

CHAPTER III SETTLEMENT OF MCINDOE FALLS; ESTABLISHMENT OF THE LUMBER BUSINESS 

A survey (1798) of the old Connecticut River road or turnpike, which followed an Indian trail, listed among the settlers on the McIndoes plain one John McIndoe, who had come from Scotland in 1784-85 and had purchased from Samuel Stevens three hundred acres in the southeast corner of the town of Barnet. His holdings included the falls on the Connecticut at that point and a house about a third of a mile above the Ryegate line. McIndoe, whose name is borne by the falls, the village, and the Academy, had as neighbor James Gilchrist, who had come from Scotland in 1777 and who was the founder of the family which, like the Gleasons, played an important rôle in the establishment and subsequent development of the school. 1

These two original settlers of McIndoe's Falls (an early spelling), McIndoe and Gilchrist, built and operated, together with their partners, Stevens Rider and Robert Somers, a sawmill on the McIndoe Falls Brook, just west of the point where it passes under Route 5. The first dam on the Connecticut between McIndoe Falls and Lyman (later Monroe), as well as the first bridge and the first mill at this location, were probably built by Calvin Palmer, the dam and the mill being built prior to 1803. A mill was also erected on the Lyman side of the river. The lumber business developed rapidly and made a major contribution to the economic and social life of the village named after John McIndoe, which, in 1829, boasted twenty-two families, a sawmill, shingle machine, clapboard mill, turner's shop, and a brick schoolhouse. The neighboring village in Lyman had twelve families, a gristmill, sawmill, two shingle machines, oil mill, clothier's works, and a carding machine.2

It is significant that John McIndoe's grandson, Lyman J. 

McIndoe, born in Barnet and later a resident of Newbury, was the publisher of newspapers at Newbury (Aurora of the Valley), Bradford (Orange County Journal), and Windsor (Vermont Jour nal, Vermont Chronicle). It was the Vermont Chronicle Press which printed in 1854 the first catalogue of McIndoe's Falls Academy, covering the year 1853-54. 3

Beginning in the 1830's, the firm of Brewer and Gilchrist controlled the lumber business, owning timber lands in Essex and Coos Counties in Vermont and New Hampshire, respectively, as well as in Pennsylvania. Some ten years later the partnership was dissolved, the Pennsylvania holdings, which included much of the early oilproducing lands of that state, being taken over by Ebenezer Brewer. 

The Gilchrists - James, Alexander, Archibald, and Walter sumed full control of the northern properties. Shortly thereafter 

Lloyd Kimball purchased an interest in the firm, and, in 1848, William Monteith became a junior partner. 4 

as- The prosperity of this lumber concern, Kimball, Gilchrist & Co., heightened by the completion in 1850 of the Connecticut and Passumpsic Railway line to McIndoe's Falls, increased the population and contributed greatly to the welfare of that village and of 10

Barnet, as well as of its sister community in New Hampshire. 5 

Steps were already being taken to improve the cultural advantages of the growing village, for, in the same year that the railroad arrived, an attractive Congregational Church was erected to house the congregation which, prior to that date, had met in the brick schoolhouse, opposite the site of the present graded school. Earlier churches erected in the vicinity were those at Barnet Center (ca. 

1784, ca. 1800, 1829, 1849), Lyman (North Monroe-ca.1824), Barnet (1829), and Walter Harvey (1831). It is not surprising, then, that the public-spirited citizens of the community, alert to educational, as well as to religious needs, were moved to establish a secondary school to provide more advanced instruction for the young people of the region.

CHAPTER IV EARLY SECONDARY EDUCATION IN VERMONT; 

THE ACADEMY MOVEMENT 

The first half of the nineteenth century witnessed the rapid growth of a new type of school in America, the academy. The decline of the Latin grammar schools of the preceding century and a half, due in part to a restricted curriculum which did not meet changing conditions, was a large factor in this educational development. In 1749, Benjamin Franklin had proposed the establishment, in Philadelphia, of an academy providing more practical training than the Latin grammar schools of the day. 

The founding of the Philadelphia Public Academy (1749), with 

Latin, English, and Mathematical courses of study, of Dummer 

Academy (1763) and Phillips Academy (1778) in Massachusetts, and of Phillips Exeter Academy (1781) in New Hampshire, were milestones in the rise of this new type of school. Seventeen such institutions had been established in Massachusetts by 1800. In the period 1826-35, sixty academies were incorporated in that state, and by 1850 there were six thousand in the whole country. 

The development of secondary education in Vermont followed substantially the pattern, established earlier in southern New England; i.e., grammar schools, academies, and high schools. 

The first constitution of the State of Vermont, adopted at Windsor July 8, 1777, recommended the establishment of one grammar school in each county. Two years later, lands for the maintenance of grammar schools were first set aside in township charters. The school law of 1782, providing for the establishment of such institutions was "permissive, rather than mandatory.” That same year the county judges were authorized to appoint trustees of county schools and to lay taxes on counties for the erection of schoolhouses. 3

One should note that "In most of the townships granted by Vermont, one right of land was reserved for the support of a grammar school or academy, but as less than half of the towns were so granted (Barnet had received a prior grant from Governor Wentworth of the Province of New Hampshire), many of the schools derived little aid from this source, and in fact the establishment of county schools was not generally effected; and though there are many grammar schools and academies in the state, few of them are endowed, but depend on the tuition fees for their support." "4 The first secondary school in Vermont was Clio Hall, established at Bennington (1780), followed by the first county grammar school-Windsor County Grammar School (Norwich, 1785), Rut land County Grammar School (Castleton, 1787), Athens Grammar 

School or Windham Hall (1791; moved to Newfane in 1801), and 

Cavendish Grammar School or Academy (1791). Andrews does not mention the founding of a grammar school at Newbury, yet Wells writes: "The first grammar school in this part of New England was opened at Newbury in the fall of 1784. This school seems to have been discontinued in 1790." One should cite Haverhill 12

(N. H.) Academy, founded in 1794 and still rendering valuable service. 5

The year 1795 marked the founding of the first secondary school in Caledonia County - Caledonia County Grammar School, later known as Peacham Academy. Chartered October 27th of that year, only three years after the organization of the county, that fine old institution, a friendly rival of McIndoes Academy for the past century, opened its doors in 1797. 6 

The academy movement gained momentum in Vermont during the first half of the nineteenth century and continued until about 1870, when the westward movement of population and the rise of the high school checked the spread of private academies. Among those founded in northeastern Vermont in 1800-1850 were: Thetford Academy (opened in 1819, it is "with the exception of Caledonia County Grammar School at Peacham, the oldest private academy still [1931] in existence in Vermont"), Bradford Academy (1820), Concord Academy (founded by Samuel R. Hall in 1823 and regarded by many as the “first normal school not only in Vermont but also in America"), Phillips Academy (Danville, 1840), and St.

Johnsbury Academy (1842), one of the outstanding schools in the state. 7

During the period 1849-53, twenty-five academies were chartered in Vermont. In 1853 the following schools received charters: Island 

Pond Academy, West River Academy (Londonderry), Hammond 

Female Institute (Fayetteville), Springfield Wesleyan Seminary,

Chittenden County Institute (Essex Center), Northern Educa tional Union (Fairfax), and McIndoes Academy, originally called 

McIndoe's Falls Academy. 8 13

CHAPTER V FOUNDING OF MCINDOES ACADEMY 

It is pertinent, before discussing the establishment of the Academy, to examine briefly educational conditions in Barnet prior to 1853. In his excellent history, Wells cites the sterling minister of the Center Church, Mr. Goodwillie, as stating that a Revolutionary 

War veteran named Wilson taught the first school in Barnet, his log schoolhouse being located between Barnet and McIndoes. Other early teachers were William Shearer, who had come from Scotland in 1784 and who taught in his home near Ross Pond, and William Johnson, also a Scot and a veteran, who conducted a school, first near the north end of Harvey's Lake and later close to the Center Church. Wells says that, in 1787, the selectmen were first instructed to hire a schoolmaster and that, in 1793, a group of citizens erected a schoolhouse near the Walter Johnson home. I 

The first division of the town into school districts occurred in 1799, a second division into ten districts occurring in 1809. Educational conditions long remained rather primitive, blackboards not being introduced until about 1810, and stoves, about 1815. The examination of teachers began in 1850, just prior to the founding of the Academy. 2

As we have seen, local economic and social conditions prevailing in the early 1850's favored the establishment of a much-needed secondary school. The lumber business at McIndoe's Falls was flourishing; and the railroad, which had reached that village in 

October, 1850, had been extended to St. Johnsbury by January. In addition to the mills at McIndoe's Falls and those at Monroe Plain (then in Lyman), there were two woolen mills, a tannery, and a carriage-sleigh shop at Barnet (Steven's Village), as well as a cardingfulling mill, a carriage-sleigh shop, and the Ide gristmill at Passumpsic, the sawmill at East Barnet (McLeran's or Norrisville), and a gristmill, carding mill, and tannery at West Barnet.3 

The population of Barnet in 1850 was 2521, the largest recorded from 1790 (477) to 1920 (1685); the census figures for the neighboring towns being: Danville (2577), Lyman (1442), Peacham (1377), Ryegate (1606), and St. Johnsbury (2758). The coming of the railroad attracted many new residents, quickened the tempo of life in the region, and facilitated its cultural, economic, and social development. 4

The brick schoolhouse for elementary grades, built prior to 1829, and the Congregational Church (1850) have been cited as evidence of the development of community spirit and cultural interest in the village lying on the terraced bank of the Connecticut and centered at the point where John McIndoe, its founder, had erected a sawmill. So it was logical for the energetic and public-spirited citizens of the village and of Barnet, Ryegate, and Monroe to establish a secondary school. 5

The first meeting of the citizens interested in the founding of an academy at McIndoe's Falls was held late in 1852, when they agreed to form an association and to raise the necessary funds. The 14

text of that agreement, as recorded in the official journal kept by the Trustees of McIndoes Academy, reads as follows: 

"We, the subscribers, hereby agree to associate ourselves together for the purpose of erecting a suitable building at McIndoes Falls (sic), and furnish the same for a Literary and Scientific Institution, to be called the McIndoes Falls Academy; and we hereby agree the sums annexed to our names, respectively, for that object, on condition, that the sum of three thousand dollars ($3,000.00) is subscribed within two months from this date. The first meeting shall be notified by any three of the members posting up a notice in some suitable place of the time and place of the meeting. The meeting, when met, shall be organized, by choosing a Chairman to preside in said meeting, and a Secretary to record the doings of the same and members shall be entitled to one vote, for every twenty-five dollars they own in the above Corporation-and no member shall be liable to pay any Assessment after he shall have paid the full amount of his Subscription."

December 7th, 1852 

This report is supplemented by a list of the twenty-eight 6 subscribers and of the amounts subscribed. Many of the individuals thus listed represented families long prominent in this region. 7 

Some two months later-February 24, 1853-"the members of the Association for the erecting of a suitable building for an Academy at this place" met, as they often did, at the counting room of Azro Dutton's store. Here the group organized "by appointing 

Jas. McArthur, 9 Chairman, W. A. Weaks, 10 Secretary." Horace

Duncan ¹¹ and James Gilchrist 12 were instructed to draft a "code of By-laws," and Lloyd Kimball 13 and Horace Duncan were elected members of a "Building Committee." 14 W. A. Weaks was later added to this Committee. A committee, elected to ascertain and examine "the terms for which can be obtained a site, or Sites for Sd Academy," included Horace Duncan, John C. Gleason, 15 James 

McArthur, Alexander Johnson, 16 and W. A. Weaks (Weeks).

The last-named committee reported to the Academy Association March 3, 1953, that there were five available sites. Two days later, the Association voted unanimously to accept "the proposal of J. W. Gilchrist, for Site No. 5, upon which to locate the Academy building." 17

The original description of the site reads as follows: 

"A Site, between the public road, and the Railroad; and north of W. R. 

Monteith's house; and land contiguous, or not, to sd. Monteith's house; and land contiguous or not, to sd. Monteith's Land owned by J. and W. Gilchrist. Price $150 per acre."

Despite Wells' statement and the common belief that this site was donated to the Association, a Warranty Deed, dated July 2, 1867, and filed at the Barnet Town Clerk's Office, shows that James and Walter Gilchrist were paid $200 for the site, which contained about two and two-thirds acres. 18 

On March 30th, the members of the Association voted: 1—“That the dimensions of the Academy building be 45 feet in breadth by 56 feet in length. 2-That the side of the building face the main street, with a (proportional) piazza. 3—That the three principal schoolrooms be furnished with fixed desks and seats." 19 

On April 4, the McIndoes Falls (sic) Academy Association voted to adopt the report of the By-laws Committee. Three of the twelve articles are reproduced below. Article 1st-The Officers of this Association shall consist of a President, Vice-President, Clerk, Treasurer, Trustees (of five members) and Collector, all of which officers shall be chosen by ballot, at each Annual Meeting, allowing one vote for each share of $25.00 and shall hold their offices one year and until others are chosen. This article was amended on April 29 to provide that the 15

Board of Trustees should consist of at least five, and not more than nine mem bers, at the discretion of the Association. Article 2nd-The Annual Meeting of this Association shall be held on the first Monday of January of each year, at the Academy building (except the first which shall be held April 18th, 1853 at 

A. Dutton's Counting Room). Article 8th-It shall be the duty of the trustees to hold all property in trust, except money, to engage Preceptor and Teachers; and manage the prudential concerns of the Association." 20 

The following officers of the Association were elected on April 

18th: Rev. James McArthur, President; Hon. James Gilchrist, Vice-President; Dr. W. A. Weaks, Clerk; Alexander Johnson, Esq., Treasurer; Alexander Johnson, Collector. The following members of the Association were elected Trustees: James Gilchrist, John C. 

Gleason, Lloyd Kimball, J. M. Child, 21 James McArthur 22

An assessment of 25% on the subscription was voted, to be paid by May 15th. On April 29th, the Association adopted an additional by-law calling for the election-without restriction as to numbers or membership-of honorary members, "for counsel."

Following this meeting the Trustees immediately convened for the first time, elected W. A. Weaks Secretary, and took steps to effect the collection of the assessment. 23

The members of the Association and the Trustees acted promptly for, at the meeting of the latter on July 20th, the Building Committee reported that "the Academy building can be in readiness for a school by the middle of September next," the Trustees then voting unanimously "that we have a School-to commence as early as the 14th day of September next." Three members, John C. Gleason,

W. A. Weaks, and James McArthur, were authorized "to engage a preceptor to teach in sd. Academy," and two members, Azro 

Dutton and Lloyd Kimball, were delegated "to secure Boarding Rooms, etc. for scholars." The Trustees then elected several honorary members of their board. 24

The Building Committee had not been idle, for work on the Academy building had been going on since spring under the direction of William Č. Martin, master carpenter. The project had the support of the whole community, like the traditional barn raisings. 

Some of the subscribers made their payments in lumber and in work, farmers using their ox teams to help dig the cellar or to draw stone for the foundation and lumber for the frame. 25 

It is likely that the Academy building cost its subscribers more than they anticipated, for it is said that John C. Gleason, one of the original group and a Trustee, tanned a thousand hides which, sold at $1 each, permitted him to contribute $1000-a sum considerably in excess of his subscription-to the building of the school. 26

One of the students of McIndoe's Falls Academy in its first year, 1853-54, was William H. Gilchrist, son of Alexander Gilchrist and nephew of James and Walter Gilchrist, who stated that the cost of the building was $5005, of which a substantial part, $2500, was the handsome gift of Dr. Francis B. Brewer, a gift which enabled the 

Academy to start its career free of debt. This seems to accord with the fact that the total amount originally subscribed was $3200. 

Yet the April, 1857 Catalogue of the Academy states that the gift was made by Mr. Brewer and that it was the basis for an endowment fund. Wells' sketch of the Brewers indicates that it was 

Ebenezer, not his son, who made the gift. 27 16

Partner of Silas C. Kelly in a stage business (Haverhill-Danville) 

and later a member of the lumber firm of Brewer & Gilchrist, Ebenezer Brewer had gone to Pennsylvania to exploit the company's land holdings there, following the dissolution of the Vermont partnership. He was soon joined by his son, Francis B., (Dartmouth, 1843; Dartmouth Medical School, 1846) who, after several years of practice in Barnet, Plymouth, Mass., and Westfield, N. Y., engaged in the lumber business with his father at Titusville, Pa. Interesting himself in the possibilities of oil found in springs on their property, Dr. Brewer was instrumental in executing there the first oil lease in the country July 4, 1853 and in convincing Dr. Dixi Crosby (Dartmouth Medical School, 1824) and George H. Bissell (Dartmouth, 1845) of the value of the oil. Their pioneer efforts culminated in the sinking of the first oil well in Titusville by Colonel Drake in 1859. A memorial tablet, unveiled at Crosby Hall (Dartmouth) June 26, 1953, celebrates "the centennial anniversary of the world's first scientific examination of crude oil." We quotein part-the inscription: "In 1853, Francis B. Brewer, class of 1843, brought a sample of Pennsylvania Rock Oil to Dartmouth for analysis." 28

Thus McIndoes Academy has, through the gift of the Brewers, whose property was "the birthplace of the petroleum industry" 29 and who had stock holdings in one of the first oil companies, a link with the development of that huge industry, whose birth paralleled its own.

Built in the style of the Greek revival architecture, then prevalent in Massachusetts and Connecticut but rare in northern New England, the two-story Academy building (45′ x 56′), with its stately Doric columns and pediment and its ornamental cupola, is indeed a dignified structure. The same Greek influence was apparent in the original buildings housing St. Johnsbury Academy and Phillips Academy (Danville). In fact, the resemblance between the latter (no longer standing) and the McIndoes Academy building was striking. 30

Beginning its long and useful career with the opening-September 14, 1853 of the first academic year of McIndoes Academy, the Academy building was, and is, a tribute to the foresight and skill of the devoted citizens and able workmen who, in a fine display of community enterprise and zeal, made possible the rapid completion of such an ambitious project and the September occupancy of this handsome structure less than seven months after the appointment of a building committee. 17

CHAPTER VI OPENING OF THE ACADEMY 

On August 20, 1853, the St. Johnsbury Caledonian carried this item: "We learn that an elegant and commodious building is being erected in the thriving village of McIndoe's Falls, as an academy. 

It will be completed in time for a school this fall, commencing about the middle of September." Three weeks later there likewise appeared this advertisement: "This new School will be opened on Wednesday, September 14 and continue eleven weeks under the instruction of E. Cleavland (sic), M.A., Principal, with able assistants. Mr. Cleavland is an able and experienced Teacher, and the Trustees feel confident that the School will take and sustain an elevated position under his charge. Tuition (per term—Ed.)—Payable during the first three weeks of the 

Term-Common English-$3.00, Higher English-$3.50, Languages-$4.00, French, Spanish, and Italian each, extra $1.00. Monochromatic, Polychromatic and other ornamental branches will be taught at the usual prices. Board and rooms for self-boarders can be obtained on reasonable terms. W. A. Weaks, Secretary. McIndoe Falls, Vt. September, 1853."

At the first fall meeting of the Board of Trustees on October 12th, favorable action was taken on the motion: "That the Academy building be used for Literary and Scientific purposes only, as per first object of subscription," í an action due to have its repercussions later. The meeting on November 10th was of special interest, for the Board then adopted the following committee report: 1 "To the Trustees of the McIndoe Falls (sic) Academy-Gentlemen, Your Committee appointed July 20th to engage a Preceptor for your Academy beg leave to report: That they have attended to the duties of this appointment, by engaging the Rev. E. Cleaveland (sic), M.A., for one term, of eleven weeks, commencing September 14, 1853 as follows. Mr. Cleaveland is to furnish himself with the requisite number of assistants to teach all the branches of Education usually taught in New England Academies, and to defray the incidental expenses of the School (for the term) and to receive as remuneration for his own and his assistants' services, and Sd expenses, the tuition accruing from sd term.-All of which is respectfully submitted, September 7th, 1853, J. C. Gleason, W. A. 

Weaks, J. McArthur-Com." 2 At this time the first Principal "presented a Code of By-laws for the regulation of the School." 

It will be noted that the first Preceptor, or Principal, of the Academy selected his assistants, served as business manager, and drew his remuneration from the balance of tuition payments left after paying the running expenses of the school. 

The first catalogue of the Academy, dated April, 1854, tells us much about the first year, listing the trustees, the instructors for 1853-54 and 1854-55, and the students, and describing the institution and the courses. The instructors listed for 1853-54 are: Rev. 

Edward Cleveland, Preceptor (fall, winter, and spring terms); Elizabeth Harvey, Preceptress (fall); Lucy A. Marsh, Preceptress (winter, spring); Mrs. William W. Thayer, Teacher of Music and Drawing (fall); Lois Sartwell, Assistant Pupil (spring). 3 

Born in Shipton, Quebec, December 9, 1804, the son of New Englanders,

Edward Cleveland was graduated from Yale (A.B., 1832), studied at Yale Divinity 

School, and preached in Connecticut and New Hampshire, one of his pastorates being in Bath (1844-49), where he married Mary Lang, daughter of Sherburne Lang. After several years in Cabot, he came to McIndoe Falls as the first Principal of the Academy (1853-54) and pastor of the Congregational Church (1853-55; Wells gives 1854-56). Later head of St. Francis College (Canada), he also preached, taught, and supervised schools in Bath and in the Middle West. 

Mr. Cleveland died in Kansas in 1886. 4 18

The first preceptress of McIndoe's Falls Academy, Elizabeth Stark Harvey, granddaughter of Alexander Harvey, founder of Barnet, and great-granddaughter of General John Stark, was educated at Peacham Academy and at Bradford (Mass.) Academy. (The latter school, now Bradford Junior College, founded in 

1803, is celebrating its 150th anniversary this year.) After her marriage to 

Frank C. Bill of Barnet, she resided at St. Johnsbury, McIndoe Falls, and 

Cambridge, Mass. The second preceptress, Lucy A. Marsh, a graduate of 

Thetford Academy, was the daughter of Rev. Joseph Marsh, who had moved to 

Thetford in 1852. She was one of the first group of girls to graduate from 

Thetford Academy (1852). Miss Marsh married George A. Dustan, second Prin cipal of the Academy. 5

The catalogue describes the building as follows: 

"The Academy building is situated a few rods from the Depot of the Passumpsic Railroad and enclosed by a neat fence, its front facing the main street, so beautifully adorned with maples. The building is two stories high, suitably divided into a commodious Hall, Recitation Rooms, Laboratory and Reading Rooms,-the whole cost of which was between five and six thousand dollars. It is already supplied with a Philosophical and Chemical Apparatus, which will be enlarged during the present year by valuable additions. It has a library of several hundred volumes, lately presented by the liberality of Henry Stevens, Esq.”6 Son of Enos Stevens, one of the early and prominent settlers of Barnet, Henry Stevens was long active in that town and in Vermont. 

Farmer, inn-keeper, mill-owner, and promoter of stage and rail lines, he became a skilled book collector and dealer and an authority on Vermontiana and Americana. Founder-in Barnet-of the Vermont Historical Society, he was its first president. His distinguished sons are mentioned by Wells. His cousin, Richard, attended the Academy in 1856-57. 7

The geographical distribution of the students listed in the first catalogue reveals the popularity of the newly-founded institution. 

The attendance is given as follows: fall term (63), winter term (54), spring term (90), summer term (45), a total of 252 registrations and 148 different students, from 16 towns in three states. In 1853 the enrollment at Peacham Academy was 183; at St. Johnsbury Academy, 184. Hiram G. Brainard of Boston was the only student not from Vermont or New Hampshire. The Vermont towns represented, in addition to McIndoe Falls (44), and Barnet (26), were Topsham (2), Ryegate (15), Peacham (2), Danville (2), St. Johnsbury (2), and Coventry, Lunenburg, and Waterford, one each. The New Hampshire towns sending students, in addition to Bath (16) and Lyman (29), were Danbury (3), New London, Littleton, and Salisbury, one each. The student from Salisbury was 

S. Everett Pingree, later Governor of Vermont and perhaps the most distinguished alumnus of the Academy. Also among the students were three Clevelands, probably the children of the Principal, as well as Robert M. Harvey and 

Caroline Harvey, cousin and sister of the first Preceptress. 8 The students, listed separately as "males" (59) and "females" (89), were classified by departments: 

Classical (12 males, 20 females), English (35 males, 58 females), Normal (14 males, 36 females), and Preparatory (12 males, 11 females.) 9

The Preparatory Department included pupils not yet in their teens who were doing elementary academic work. On the other hand, many of the students in the other departments were in their twenties, young men and women who were striving to make up their educational deficiencies. It was then quite customary for students, either for financial reasons or because of the heavy demands of farm and home duties, to attend school only one or two terms a year, thus spreading their secondary school education over a longer period than now.

Although there is reason to believe that the first formal graduating exercises were not held until 1898, it should be pointd out that the first catalogue contains this statement: "Any one can Graduate who has completed the regular course of Study with credit, and receive a Diploma with the signature of the Principal and the Trustees." Three members of the Classical Department (which em- 19

phasized college preparation) in 1853-54, including S. Everett

Pingree, are listed as graduates. The award of diplomas and formal graduation at St. Johnsbury Academy did not occur prior to 1860, whereas diplomas were first awarded to graduating students at 

Peacham Academy in 1898. 10

The course of study of the Classical and English Departments covered three years, Junior, Middle, and Senior, the work being carried on during four terms (fall-11 weeks, winter-10 weeks, spring-11 weeks, summer-10 weeks). In the Classical Department, the emphasis, aside from short courses in algebra, Roman history, Roman antiquities, and mythology, was definitely laid on Greek and Latin authors, such as Sallust, Cicero, Xenophon, Virgil and Homer. The 

English Department offered a wide selection of courses, ranging from arithmetic, English grammar, algebra, and history to bookkeeping, botany, astronomy, chemistry, rhetoric, moral science, trigonometry, English poets, and logic. The sale of books was handled by the Principal. Many of the same books were used at Peacham Academy in 1860 and at St. Johnsbury Academy during its early years. 11 Students in the Normal Department were given “instruction in the 

Theory and Practice of Teaching," were members of a Model Class," and were awarded certificates after a “thorough examination.” Many of the students—and this was true for a long period-were preparing for a career in teaching, particularly in the elementary schools. One of the earliest alumnae was Jane E. 

Sutherland, a district school teacher in McIndoe Falls where she was long a resident. 12 The Preparatory Department offered courses in reading, arithmetic, geography, and U. S. history. 

There was a wholesome emphasis upon religion at the Academy then, as later, for the catalogue states: "All members of the school are required to attend public worship on the Sabbath at such places as parents or guardians may designate and also upon the daily devotional exercises of the Academy. They are also required to attend a Biblical exercise Sabbath morning of term time." 13

In each department, rhetorical exercises and compositions were held every Wednesday. In the Classical Department, original declamations were required in the last year.

Wells speaks of the formation of a literary society at the Academy in September, 1854. The first catalogue carries this reference to that organization: “a literary Society each week." He also cites a catalogue of the society as listing a total membership of fifty-nine young men and women. It conducted a lyceum where lectures, debates, and Shakespearean readings were given and enjoyed by students and townspeople alike, thus preparing the former for the end-of-term exhibitions and providing the latter with a preview of modern adult education courses. The cultural influence of such activities must have been widespread. 14 

Mention should also be made of the stress laid on the “ornamental branches," such as monochromatic and polychromatic painting, crayon flowers, wax flowers, papier-mâché, etc., then considered an important part of feminine education. 

The Code of Bylaws prepared by the Principal is prefaced by this statement: "As the office of this Institution is to afford instruction to young persons of both sexes in all the branches usually taught in New England Academies, and to exert the best influence in reference to their character, usefulness and happiness, the following arrangements shall be observed to accomplish it." 15 Then follows a list of nineteen regulations governing "Terms and Vacations, Government and Instruction, Admission and Duty of the Students, Tuition and Examina. tions," etc.

As indicated above, many of the students came a considerable distance to attend the Academy. The service on the Passumpsic 

Railroad consisted of two trains each way per day ("Down Trains,” 20

and 11 A. M., “Up Trains,” 4 and 9 P.M.) The morning hours scheduled could not have been convenient for students. 16 

Since it was necessary for many students to secure accommodations in town, the Trustees took appropriate action, the 1853-1854 Catalogue stating: "A large and commodious Boarding House is in process of erection this Summer which will be opened for the Fall Term (1854-55). Good accommodations can be obtained in the 

Village for such as may wish to board themselves. The house mentioned was used as a student boarding house for many years, according to Mrs. Harriet L. Kendall, a student at the Academy in 

1887-89. Later the residence of W. H. Gilchrist, it still stands directly south of the Academy. Part of the building was soon detached and moved to become a part of the Paddleford House, once used, it is said, by self-boarders. Readers will learn with envy that the board was, "per week (including room, washing, fuel, and lights), $1.622 to $2.00," 17 the average price of a dinner a century later.

In order to orient the opening of the Academy to the life of the period, we should note that the revered Hiram Orcutt was then Headmaster of Thetford Academy, with some 200 students; Joseph 

King was presiding over Newbury Seminary and Female Collegiate 

Institute; the able James K. Colby was Principal of St. Johnsbury

Academy, and Thomas S. Pearson was the head of Peacham Academy. A current best-seller was Harriet Beecher Stowe's Uncle Tom's 

Cabin; Horace Mann was about to become President of Antioch 

College; a delegation of Iroquois Indians from Canada was shortly to visit Montpelier to present their claims to Vermont lands north of the Onion (Winooski) River; Franklin Pierce was President;

Erastus Fairbanks had just completed his first term as Governor of Vermont; and the slavery problem was assuming alarming proportions. 18 127

CHAPTER VII GRANTING OF THE CHARTER; THE FIRST YEAR 

The signal event of the first academic year, the granting of a charter to the Academy by the General Assembly of the State of Vermont, occurred on December 1, 1853. Entitled "An Act to 

Incorporate the McIndoe Falls Academy," the charter (see Appendix) lists the following: James Gilchrist, John C. Gleason, Walter 

Gilchrist, William A. Weaks, Azro Dutton, Samuel Gleason, 2

Charles Peabody, 3 Luther F. Parker, 4 Simeon Heath, 5 James 

McArthur, John M. Child, William R. Monteith, 6 and Horace

Duncan, their associates and successors who 66 'are constituted a body politic and corporate-for the purpose of maintaining a literary and scientific institution to be known and called by the name of McIndoes Falls Academy. The persons named in the first section of this act are hereby constituted a board of Trustees for the government and management of the interests and concerns of said Academy and may hold and discharge the duties of said trustees until others shall be appointed trustees." 7 

Thus the new school, so efficiently and promptly established by its enterprising founders, acquired legal recognition and sanction. 

Two days after the granting of the charter, the Caledonian announced the opening of the winter term on December 5th, “under the care of the present Principal, Rev. E. Cleveland, M.A., assisted by Miss L. A. Marsh, with such others as the interest of the school demands." Undoubtedly the close of the fall term was marked by an exhibition or public examination. Such an event, characteristic of the period, was announced, both in the catalogue and in the bylaws, for the end of the fall and spring terms. 

In conformity with the charter, a meeting of the McIndoes Falls Academy Association was held December 31st for the purpose of organizing, James McArthur being elected President and Lloyd Kimball, Secretary pro tem. The meeting was then adjourned until January 2, 1854, when it was voted that they were "willing to accept of their Special Charter received from the Vermont Legislature." 8 The "constituted Trustees under Special Charter" then met, electing W. A. Weaks Secretary. It was voted, unanimously, to "Accept and Adopt our Special Charter," and voted to "recognize as our Associates all the persons of the former Association whose names are not mentioned in the Act of incorporation" and to "readopt the terms of the first subscription and the By-laws and proceedings both of Association and of trustees heretofore adopted by them under the general act of the Statute of the State of 

Vermont." 9 The Association then elected the following Officers and Trustees: 

James McArthur, President; James Gilchrist, Vice-President; W. A. 

Weaks, Clerk; Alexander Johnson, Treasurer and Collector; 

Trustees: James Gilchrist, John C. Gleason, John M. Child, Lloyd

Kimball, Azro Dutton, Horace Duncan, Samuel Gleason. The 

Treasurer reported receipts of $2,878.50, expenditures of $2,872.29, and a balance of $6.21. Horace Duncan was elected Auditor. "The building committee reported that the expense of preparing and fencing Ground, and purchasing same; building house; supplying furniture, etc. has been as nearly as they can estimate it from their present means for doing so 22

$5,005.00," that "the Association are (sic) now in debt for above-$2,162.27," that "there are assets in unpaid subscriptions to the amount of $287.50, leaving a balance to be provided for when all the subscriptions are paid in of $1,874.77. 10 This is probably the debt liquidated by the generous Brewer gift. The Trustees concluded this busy evening by voting to approve the proceedings of the old Association met this January 2nd, 1854 in electing the officers of the Association and we hereby adopt them as the proceedings and 

Officers of this corporation." 11

Meeting at Azro Dutton's counting-room on January 16, 1854, the Trustees appointed John C. Gleason and Horace Duncan "a committee to ascertain the qualifications of Mr. George Dustan (Andover Theological Seminary) as a Teacher," and also a committee "on the use of the Academy building for a Singing School." 

The use of the Academy building by outside organizations often a thorny problem was already vexing the Trustees, for they asked Lloyd Kimball to confer with Mr. Cleveland "on the subject of 

Mr. Cushing's lecturing in the Academy." 12

The minutes of the February 6th meeting contain the following contract with George Duştan, who was to succeed Mr. Cleveland at the opening of the summer term in 1854. "The Trustees of McIndoe Falls (sic) Academy propose to give the use of the building and property belonging to it (however reserving so much of the use of the hall and a passage to it as is desired by the Trustees for Library, Scientific and Useful Lectures and purposes at all times, not however disturbing to the school) to Mr. G. Dustan for the purpose of his entering and continuing a school at said Academy to commence at the summer term next ensuing, Mr. 

Dustan being at all the expense necessary to induce attendance and carry on the institution to apparent beneficial results and prosperity. The Trustees reserve the right to discontinue and put an end to any contract at the close of any quarter and Mr. Dustan has the privilege of ending his obligations to the 

Trustees in like manner." 13 

John C. Gleason and Rev. James McArthur signed for the

Trustees and Lucy A. Marsh (soon to be Mrs. Dustan) signed for 

George Dustan. 

The spring term opened on February 24th, "under the care of 

Mr. E. Cleveland, as Principal." 14 The conclusion of the winter term had been the occasion of an important event, the dedication of the Academy, described in the Caledonian of March 11th. "The friends and patrons of this Institution have just cause for congratulation at the success of their laudable enterprise. It is less than a year since they first entered into a plan of the establishment. They now have a splendid building, fifty-six feet in length, forty-five feet in breadth, two stories high containing eight well-finished rooms, besides a large cellar. Their chemical and philosophical apparatus is well selected and their library shelves are filled with choice and rare works. The cost of the builing was $5,000. "This Seminary is located in the midst of a large region of country, whose population is principally agricultural and mechanical, ready and willing to patronize education in all its important branches. The wisdom and liberality of its patrons are visible in the site they have chosen, the selection they have made for their Principal and the expense they have incurred for this worthy object. Everything seems favorable for the realization of their highest expectation. "It was the peculiar privilege of the writer of this article to be present at the interesting service closing the winter term of this Institution. It was designed to serve two objects:-the dedication of the building and close of the term. 

The exercises were exceedingly appropriate. The opening prayer was offered by Rev. Mr. McArthur. Then a very able and eloquent address was delivered by the accomplished Principal, Rev. Mr. Cleveland, the prayer, by Rev. Mr. Kimball. "After a recess of a few minutes, a sumptuous collation was furnished to the entire assembly, containing everything which a refined epicure might desire. 23

A short and very appropriate address by Rev. Mr. Kimball followed, accompanied with interesting remarks by Rev. Mr. McArthur, Hon. James Gilchrist,

Henry Stevens, Esq., and others. The whole was interspersed with choice singing by the choir which merited special praise. The splendor of the scene was much increased by paintings and evergreen decorations, the work of the delicate and skillful hands of the ladies. "There are many reasons why this Academy should be extensively patronized by the public, some of which are: its quiet location, its distance from other like institutions, ability of the board of instructors, liberality of its patrons and determination of its board of Trustees to make it everything which the comfort and improvement of its pupils require. A Spectator. McIndoes Falls, February 17, 1854." 15

The Academy and those associated with it have always been closely linked with the well-being of the towns it serves. As early as April 22, 1854, the Caledonian announced the "Spring Examination of Teachers" (elementary) to be held in Steven's Village. Of the three supervisors of public schools in Barnet that year, two were James McArthur and Edward Cleveland, the other, Rev. A. O. Hubbard.

The youths of the day had their fun and their escapades. Charles 

Gleason, brother of Horace, has heard his father tell of a practical joke which involved greasing the stairway so that the Principal, hurrying to catch some culprits, made a quick, but unorthodox, descent to the first floor. On another occasion, the Principal detected a strange odor rising from the cellar and found some boys popping corn in the furnace. Seeking a quick escape, they tried to scramble through the narrow cellar windows. One negotiated the passage; the other stuck fast and was hauled back into the cellar, where he observed the application of heat to another surface. 

The summer term opened on May 29th under the direction of 

George Dustan, A.B., 16 with Lucy A. Marsh, Preceptress; Joseph T. 

Brown, Assistant Teacher; Emma Cheever (Preparatory Department); Mrs. Mary_Cleveland, wife of the first Principal (Italian, Spanish); Laura C. Dustan (Music), Orinda G. Kimball 17 (Oil 

Painting, Wax Fruit, and Flowers); Andrew Sargent (Penmanship,

Pen Drawing, and Practical Surveying); and Joseph Manchester, Librarian. 18 

This summer term concluded the first full academic year of the Academy's existence, auspiciously begun, for much had been accomplished since the meeting of the subscribers. The towns most concerned and, indeed, the whole region had abundant reason to be proud of this new institution. 24

CHAPTER VIII THE EARLY YEARS OF THE ACADEMY 

The first year having been a decided success, the Academy continued its progress in 1854-55 under the experienced direction of 

George Dustan, aided by Misses Marsh, Cheever, Dustan, and

Kimball, and Messrs. Brown, Manchester, Sargent, and W. W. 

Stevens (Assistant Teacher). 1 

It should be pointed out that teachers and assistants frequently served only one or two terms. The annual Report of the State Secretary of Education lists three male and three female teachers for that year and an enrollment of 208 different students, the highest recorded. The Academy opened its doors for the second year September 6th, some two months after the town of Monroe was formally separated (July 13, 1854) from Lyman. 2 An item in the 

Caledonian of August 12th reads as follows: 

"Such as pass through a full course and sustain a satisfactory examination will receive diplomas signed by the Officers of the Institution." 

On October 28th the first meeting of the Caledonia County School 

Association was held at St. Johnsbury, Edward Cleveland and 

George Dustan being elected to posts in the organization. The second meeting was "held in the spacious hall of the elegant and commodious Academy at McIndoes Falls" March 17, 1855, Edward 

Cleveland presiding. 3

At the January meeting of the Association, W. A. Weaks was elected Secretary and ways of "extinguishing the indebtedness of the Association were discussed."4 The wedding of George Dustan and 

Lucy Marsh occurred on February 14th. 5 The following week the 

Caledonian announced the opening of the spring term as follows: "The Boarding House is now opened for the reception of students and ample accommodations may be had either there or in private homes in the village. 

Good rooms for self-boarders are provided near the Academy." 

Meeting in Azro Dutton's counting-room on April 16th, the Trustees elected this Visiting Committee "to examine the School and Teachers as often as they think expedient: Rev. E. Irving Carpenter (Littleton), Rev. A. G. House (Passumpsic), Rev. Francis Warriner (Waterford), Rev. C. W. Cushing (Newbury), and the Hon. Joshua Stevens (Hardwick)"'; contracted with George Dustan “to remain as Principal of the Academy" until September, 1856; and, shortly thereafter, empowered Trustees John C. Gleason, W. A. Weaks, and James Gilchrist "to solicit further subscriptions" to extinguish "the present indebtedness of the 

Corporation." 6

A summer-term advertisement in the Caledonian reads: "Board, $1.85 per week, exclusive of washing, or if any prefer, the charge for board, exclusive of washing, and tuition in the English branches, will be $20 per quarter."

The same paper carried on July 21st a resolution signed by S. S. 

Taylor and S. B. Page of the "C.R.L. Society of the McIndoe's Falls 

Academy, on the death of James Andrew Aldrich of Concord, Vt."

Possibly "C. R. L." stood for Connecticut River Literary Society. 

The fall term of eleven weeks opened August 29, 1855, with

George and Lucy Dustan in charge of the school. In addition to 

Misses Dustan and Kimball, Charles Allen, Elocutionist, and Jacob Dalpé, A.B.,7 Teacher of French and Surveying, were on the faculty. “The assistance of Mons. Dalpé, a native Frenchman and recent graduate of 

Dartmouth College, has been secured. Much time will be devoted to conversa- 25

tion in French. Tuition in French extra, only $2.00. Those who desire it can obtain instruction in Penmanship and Vocal Music." 8 The third academic year was also marked by a good attendance, 192 different students being registered. 9 

The traditional annual meeting of the Association was held at the Academy January 3, 1856. Deacon James Gilchrist was instructed "to consult legal authority in regard to the power of the Association to collect the balance of the uncollected subscriptions." The 

Trustees then "elected L. Kimball and Rev. Jas. McArthur a committee to rent the Hall." 10

The spring term opened in late February, with Mrs. S. S. Cargill as Teacher of Instrumental Music. Later issues of the Caledonian announced the appointment of Edward Cleveland as Principal of Barton Academy, the ordination of his successor, Benjamin F. 

Ray, as pastor of the Congregational Church, the election of Henry Stevens, friend and benefactor of the Academy, as moderator of the Barnet town meeting, and the meeting of the stockholders (Edward Cleveland, Clerk) of the Connecticut and Passumpsic Rivers Railroad at the St. Johnsbury House (opened in 1850). 

Obliged to replace George Dustan, who had returned to Andover to resume his theological studies, the Trustees made a contract with 

Sylvanus (Silvanus) Hayward, 11 A.B. for 1856-57. James Gilchrist and Horace Duncan were authorized to "superintend the painting of the Academy fence, cleanse and repair the buildings, etc." 12 

The Catalogue for 1856-57 described the first year of Hayward's service as Preceptor. Now holding an A.M. degree, he had, as his teachers Mary J. Chase, 13 Preceptress, Mrs. S. S. Ray 14 (Music), Caroline Harvey 15 (Drawing-Painting), Hannah L. Chase, 16 

Assistant (Music-Spring), Joshua Gill, 17 Assistant (Vocal Music), and George Ela 18 (Penmanship). The enrollment declined somewhat in 1856-57, 145 different students being registered from—save one from Lowell, Mass.-twenty-three New Hampshire and Vermont communities. The attendance by terms was: summer-26; fall-59; winter-46; spring-79, an aggregate attendance of 210. 19

Several passages in the Catalogue deserve mention: 

"The great aim of the Teachers in this Institution will be thoroughness, that the scholars may know every lesson.. Scholars thus drilled become eminently qualified for teachers. It is determined that the modus operandi of this Institution shall be entirely free from 'humbug', so prevalent at the present time.— 

We also ask parents to discourage the practice of going home on Friday night. 

By this means, the student loses half a day from school. (Saturday morning classes are still common in boarding schools. A. D. G.) Particular attention will be given to the Classical Department. The best advantages are offered for those wishing to fit themselves for College, either as Freshmen or in advance. Board, in good families, $2.00 per week." 20 

In September, the Trustees voted "to purchase a new furnaceon the credit of the Academy fund." A winter-term announcement in the Caledonian of November 15th reads: "The building has been newly fitted up. A fund raised places it (the Academy) upon a firmer basis." The fund mentioned probably included the 

Brewer gift. The fall had been a difficult one, since the Trusteesin addition to facing the recurring problem of indebtedness, had almost lost Principal Hayward, tempted by a "much more lucrative position in Topsfield, Mass." 21

The Trustees initiated in October a step designed to give the Academy a larger scope and to aid the community in meeting the 26

responsibilities of public education by electing James Gilchrist and 

Lloyd Kimball a "Committee to concoct, draft, and propose to the Trustees and School District (local) the terms and regulations by which a certain number of the Scholars of Sd district can attend the Academy School, by the Association receiving a certain amount of the public school funds therefore." 22 A week later "the Committee made an informal report of progress to the effect that they could consider it advisable to receive all scholars over nine years of age, residing within sd district, during the winter and summer terms (only) by their paying the regular tuition fee and that sd scholars receive so much of the public funds as sd district may think it proper to appropriate towards paying for sd Tuition." 23 

It is likely that a shortage of public-school teachers and also the reduced enrollment at the Academy during the winter and summer terms were factors in this recommendation. It was then a common practice for the academies to accept elementary pupils. 

In January, 1857, the Trustees discussed "the advisability of mortgaging the Property of the Academy for its indebtedness," but took no action. Five months later they increased the tuition for the common English branches to $3.50, higher English branches, $4.00, and ancient languages, $5.00. Favorable action was taken in July on Preceptor Hayward's proposal that the Trustees pay him $150 over and above the anticipated income from tuition, exclusive of music and drawing, the Preceptor to continue his responsibility for providing teachers, etc. 24 

The Academy opened for its fifth year September 2nd, being one of twelve Vermont secondary schools advertised in the Caledonian. 

In addition to Preceptor Hayward, Preceptress Chase (fall, winter), and 

Caroline Harvey, Assistant Pupil (Drawing, Painting-summer, fall), the faculty comprised Lois A. Sartwell, Preceptress (spring), Miss I. R. Cameron, Assistant 

Pupil (winter, spring), Orinda G. Kimball (Music—summer), Lamoille A. Grow (Music-fall), Orris Paddock (Music-winter, spring), Clinton Rowell (Penmanship—fall), Milo J. Somers (Penmanship—spring), and J. Calvin Cutter,

M.D. (Physiology-fall). The Misses Cameron, Harvey, Rowell, and Somers were students. Dr. Cutter was probably the author of Cutter's Physiology, a text used that year. 25

The total number of registrations for the year was 224, representings 156 different students from twenty-seven communities in four states, including two from Massachusetts and three from New York. 

Classes were so numerous during Preceptor Hayward's régime that recitations were held in the Buchanan House just north of the Academy. One of the New York students was Lewis L. Bullions of 

West Milton, a relative of Rev. Alexander Bullions who, like James

McArthur, had figured in the controversy which split the local Presbyterian congregations. 26 The latter's daughter, a student in the 

English Department, later attended Monmouth College. 27 George 

B. Rowell "graduated and entered Dartmouth College." 28 Another student was Francis J. Bachop of West Barnet, a descendant of 

John Bachop, Barnet pioneer, after whom Harvey's Lake and Harvey's Mountain were first named. Several days after the opening of the fall term, Miss Watkins of Baltimore addressed an anti-slavery meeting in the village. 29 

At the January, 1858 meeting of the Association, Rev. Benjamin

F. Ray 30 was elected President (Mr. McArthur resigned his Ryegate pastorate in 1858), and the Preceptor, Vice-President. Lloyd Kimball was made Clerk, replacing Dr. Weeks. The latter, who moved to 

Walden, became an Honorary Trustee. New Trustees elected were: 27

Joseph Finlay, 31 and Samuel A. Moore 32 of McIndoe Falls. Rev.

A. H. House replaced Mr. Boardman as Honorary Trustee. 33 

After the summer term, Hayward left for his new position at 

Pembroke Academy, being replaced by Richard C. Stanley, 34 A.B. 

Miss Sartwell continued as Preceptress, Miss Paddock (fall) and Mrs. Ray (winter, spring) taught Music, and Miss Harvey, Art. 

The new assistants were: Amos B. Kinney (Vocal Music-fall), Greenleaf G. Baker (Vocal Music-spring), and Isabelle Gillis, Assistant Pupil (fall, spring). 35

The fall term opened September 1, 1858, shortly after the com pletion of the first Atlantic Cable. The Caledonian of July 24th carried this announcement, reflecting a shift in educational em phasis at the Academy: 

"While special care will be given to the Classical Department,-it will also be the aim to furnish the means of acquiring a thorough and practical business education. For which purpose, particular attention will be given to the Natural 

Sciences, Bookkeeping, Surveying and Mercantile and Mechanical Studies in general. A Teachers' Class will be formed for the special benefit of those intending to teach the coming year.”

The more mature students frequently taught in the district schools during the winter, one reason for the slump in attendance at that time.

The enrollment for 1858-59 declined to 125, students coming from twenty-two towns in four states. 36 The slump was perhaps due to the business panic in 1857. The reciprocity treaty with 

Canada, admitting Canadian lumber free of duty, and the construction of the Grand Trunk Railway through the local timber holdings on the Nulhegan River had an adverse effect on New England lumber interests, many firms-including Kimball, Gilchrist & Company-going bankrupt. In addition, a disastrous fire, caused by a spark from a wood-burning locomotive, destroyed six buildings near the depot. The enrollment at St. Johnsbury Academy that year was 218, at Peacham Academy, 121. 37

A spring term announcement lays continuing stress on the "Practical and useful branches of education," but adds: "Music, French, and Ornamentals will be taught by expert Instructors." 38 

Following the departure of Mr. Stanley, Peleg Wadsworth, Jr., A.B., was engaged as Principal for 1859-60. The early fall was marked by the completion of the West Barnet Church, a balloon ascension at the St. Johnsbury Fair, and John Brown's Raid, as reported in the Caledonian. The new Principal was assisted by Hannah B. 

Corey, Preceptress (Painting and Drawing), and Mrs. Ray (Musicfall). In the winter and spring terms, Mrs. C. H. Clark of the St. 

Johnsbury Academy of Music succeeded Mrs. Ray, 39 a winter-term announcement in the Caledonian stressing the "exceptional opportunities for the study of Music and the ornamental branches." The enrollment for the three terms totaled 128 different students from twenty-one communities in Maine, New Hampshire, New York,

Vermont, and Canada. The list of texts carries several German texts for the first time. 40 A series of lectures by J. S.

Adams, Secre tary of the Vermont Board of Education marked the winter term. 41 

The spring term was well attended, 84 students being registered.

42 

The Caledonian of May 11 reported: 

"The closing examination of the school-occurred on Tuesday—and was, in every respect, both pleasing to the critics who attended and to the pupils. The 28

Trustees are very fortunate in securing the services of the present popular board of teachers for the approaching season." 

The Records, however, indicate that Mr. Wadsworth stayed only one year and that the summer term was omitted, both circumstances probably reflecting the increasing tension of the quarrel between the North and the South. 43 The date above was that of the fateful
