CHAPTER IX CIVIL WAR; POST-WAR YEARS 

For the next few years the information concerning enrollment, courses, and faculty is scanty. The war years were unsettled and rigid economies were in order. The gold fever in the 50's and, later, the decline of sheep raising, the opening of fertile lands in the West, and a rapid industrial development-these and other factors caused a shift of population to the western states and to the cities. 

The population of Barnet declined from 2,521 in 1850 to 2,002 in 

1860.1 (1860: Lyman, 743; Monroe, 619). No catalogues have been found for the period between 1860 and 1885; probably few, if any, catalogues were published during much of this period. It is also possible that certain attics, into which the author had neither the time nor the invitation to venture, contain such valuable items. 

One of the features of village life enjoyed by our ancestors during the long winter months was the local spelling-bee, a contest offering educational, gastronomic, and social advantages, when combined with an oyster, chicken pie, or box supper. Such a bee was held at Stevens Village in the winter of 1861, over 100 pupils taking part in the affair, their skill doubtless reflecting long association with Webster's "Blue-Back" Speller, or its equivalent. 2

The growing tension between the North and the South reached the explosive stage that spring, the Caledonian of April 19th announcing the attack on Fort Sumter. President Lincoln called for volunteers and Governor Fairbanks, a former resident of Barnet, proclaimed a special session of the Vermont Legislature. The war years had their inevitable effects on all phases of life and certainly on the Academy, for many alumni, students, and prospective students were in military service. James S. Kent (1853-57) was killed at Gettysburg; Calvin M. Dewey (1856-60) and Robert G. McLam (ca. 1860) died in military service, and there were others who gave their lives for the preservation of the Union. (See Appendix.)

Among those who went forth to serve their country in time of war and returned to serve it in time of peace were the following wellknown residents of the region: D. R. Gilchrist, W. H. Gilchrist, James H. Guthrie, Robert S. Kelley, William Lindsay, and 

John S. T. Wallace. 3 

But the Academy went on, opening August 28, 1861, with Josiah 

H. Hunt, 4 A.B., as Principal. Winter and spring term announcements in the Caledonian stressed instruction in music. Instruction in military science was offered at Newbury Seminary by a Norwich University captain. Two interesting local events in 1862 were the ordination of Moses Bradford's son, James, as a chaplain in the 12th Connecticut Regiment, and a military parade at McIndoe Falls, staged by a company of recruits from St. Johnsbury commanded by Henry H. Dewey (1856-1858), later a captain in the 

Wilderness Campaign. 5 

The meetings of the Board were held infrequently during the war, but the Records do provide the names of the Principals. It is known that Principal Hunt served through the spring term of 30

1862, a Mr. Gould 6 heading the school in the summer term. 7

Caroline Harvey continued as Preceptress that year. 8 

Interesting local and national events in 1862-63 were these: the concert given at St. Johnsbury by the Wesleyan Glee Club, then on a walking tour of the White Mountains; the destruction by fire of the Green Mountain House at Barnet; the Emancipation Proclamation; a second disastrous fire at Barnet; Wendell Phillips' lecture, "Reconstruction," at St. Johnsbury; and the Battle of Gettysburg. The Caledonian later carried references to "copperhead" ("secesh") ruffians in the region, an item about the Corinth Copper Company, and a description of the banquet given at Peacham for returning veterans. That fall occurred the St. Albans Raid, causing much alarm. The Norwich University Cadets patrolled Vermont and muskets were shipped to St. Johnsbury to arm the citizens. 

In September, 1863, Ephraim Eliphalet Pearson Abbott, A.B., became Principal, probably with Miss Harvey as Preceptress. 10 

He continued as Principal for the fall term of 1864-65, leaving then to begin his studies at Union Theological Seminary. He was replaced by Isaac L. Heath 11 who remained only one term. The latter's successor was Charles A. Bunker, 12 A.B. (later A.M.), who began his duties in the spring term of 1865. One of a long line of 

Dartmouth-trained Principals, he carried on superlatively the influ ence of that institution in the Upper Connecticut Valley. The spring was marked by Lee's surrender and the assassination of Lincoln. 

Locally it witnessed the drowning of Lloyd Kimball and a reception given by Walter Brock to the returning Barnet veterans. 13 

When the fall term of 1865-66 opened, Principal Bunker was assisted by Martha E. E. Bradford, 14 Preceptress, and D. A. French (Music). Miss Bradford was the daughter of Moses Bradford. There was a flurry of oil speculation that fall and several companies were formed in the county for the exploitation of so-called oil-bearing lands in Ontario, the McIndoes Falls Company purchasing or leasing lands in Bothwell, Ontario. 15 The companies had but little success, the effect on local business being adverse. During the winter of 1865-66 the Caledonian reported the discovery of gold in Lower Waterford, a lecture, “Fashions and Folly," and full-time activity at the Barnet mills.

Early in February the Trustees elected Walter Gilchrist and Samuel Gleason to fill the vacancies left by the deaths of Lloyd 

Kimball and John C. Gleason and chose William R. Monteith as

Clerk. They later engaged Principal Bunker for the ensuing year, contracting for a term payment of $37, in addition to "the usual terms." His contemporary at St. Johnsbury Academy was Henry C. 

Ide, a native of Barnet and a graduate of that school and of Dartmouth (1866), who became Governor-General of the Philippines and Minister to Spain. 16 The summer term of 1867 marked the end of C. A. Bunker's all-too-brief administration, as he left to become head of Peacham Academy, where he had a long and distinguished career. An interesting sidelight on the sports of the day was given that summer by the Caledonian, reporting a state-wide baseball tournament at Burlington. Scores were high, one at St. Johnsbury being: Olympians 46, Conn. River 21. At the county fair two teams of "oldsters" demonstrated the "round ball" game of the 30's. 

One of Mr. Bunker's classmates at Dartmouth, Daniel M. Elliot, ¹7 31

A.B. (later A.M.), was chosen as his successor. Mr. Elliot promised to keep the school two years or find a substitute. He was assisted by Sarah A. Child, Preceptress, and Eliza D. Gleason (Oils, Water 

Colors, and Pencilling), schoolmates at the Academy in 1859-60.

The former was the daughter of Deacon John M. Child. The latter, daughter of John C. Gleason, was a graduate of Abbot Academy. 18,

The tempo of life was certainly slower in the 60's than now, but it had its compensations. The Caledonian reported in February, 1868: "A social festival is to be held at Academy Hall, McIndoes. (This is the first use of "McIndoes" noted by the author.) . . . A 

Play, oyster supper, and music as special attractions." That month

Presidents Angell of Vermont and Mitchell of Middlebury were speakers on the St. Johnsbury Academy lecture course. In the spring the new Methodist Conference Seminary, replacing Newbury Seminary, was opened at Montpelier. 

In the fall of 1868, Daniel Elliot was assisted by Mary C. Bradford, sister of Martha. 19 After completing the term, he resumed his medical studies at Dartmouth. Many a college and professional career. was thus interrupted so that students could earn their way by teaching. His successor was Henry Ruthven Monteith, 20 then a 

Senior at Dartmouth, who was the first graduate of the Academy to become its Principal. 21

In November, the Caledonian described an entertainment given by the village ladies at the hotel (later the boarding house of the Connecticut Valley Lumber Company, it stood just west of the depot), where they presented the comedy of "Box and Cox." All were "invited in the costumes of olden time.” An old-fashioned supper was served, music was furnished by the Barnet Band. Admis sion was $.20; supper, $.50.

The winter-term advertisement in the Caledonian announced: "Particular attention will be paid to those intending to fit them for teaching. Bookkeeping and music can be had on reasonable terms." Two local residents, Sartwell and Worthen, were given a contract for erecting a soldiers' monument at Peacham on the original site of the Caledonia County Grammar School. This year the "upper" woolen mill at Barnet was destroyed by fire. 22 This fact, plus the general panic of that year, may have contributed to the slump in the enrollment to 75. 23

In August the Trustees signed a contract with Mr. Monteith for 

1869-70, the school opening only a few days after President and Mrs. Grant passed through Wells River en route to the White 

Mountains. 24 At the January, 1870 meeting of the Association, Mr.

Bradford, who had terminated his pastorate in McIndoe Falls the previous year, resigned the presidency. His pastoral successor, Rev. 

Submarinus G. Norcross 25 was elected head of the Association.

Unable to secure the services of Mr. Monteith for 1870-71, the 

Trustees engaged Thomas M. Martin, 26 then a high-ranking 

Senior at Dartmouth, as Principal. 27

On the roll of distinguished alumni of the Academy are the names of George A. Gates 28, college president, and Theophilus:

Grout 29, newspaper publisher, who attended the school during this period. 32

CHAPTER X THE ACADEMY IN THE 70's 

The Trustees and the Academy were fortunate in the choice of 

Thomas Martin as Principal, for he was a born teacher, musical and scholarly in his tastes, and one who exercised a fine influence in the region. The fact that he served the school as Principal during three different periods (1870-73; 1876-spring; 1891-93) indicates the esteem in which he was held. 

His contract provided for "three terms of eleven weeks each, sd Martin to do all the advertising and find himself in wood and all necessary expenses without cost to the trustees, sd Martin to have full control of the Academy building and ground . . . and have for his personal benefit that may accrue from the rent of the Hall for public Lectures or public amusing of an intellectual and moral character." 1

The rôle of the main local industry, the lumber company, was important in the life of the Academy during its first half-century. 

Following the panic of 1857 and the bankruptcy of the firm, Deacon James Gilchrist, named assignee, was able to liquidate all claims. 

The new owners of the property were Andrew Nutter, Austin Goodell (the local G. A. R. Post bore his name), and Frank Bedell, who ran the business for some eight years, sawing lumber and floating logs down the Connecticut. About 1867 Stephen Barker operated the mill for two years, the holdings being transferred to the Maineowned firm of Barker, Estabrook, and Bowman, "under the name of the McIndoe Falls Lumber Company." 2 In the fall of 1870, the 

Caledonian reported that the Company was shipping 140 carloads of lumber a month and employing 60 men. 

The official State reports list Principal Martin, two feminine assistants, and an enrollment of 121 different students. 3 At the "Annual Meeting of the Trustees and others of the McIndoes Falls 

Academy Association" early in January, 1871, Rev. Mr. Norcross was re-elected President, the following persons being chosen Trustees by ballot: Rev. M. B. Bradford, Rev. S. G. Norcross, James 

Gilchrist, Azro Dutton, Walter Gilchrist, Wm. R. Monteith, Samuel

Gleason. James Gilchrist and Monteith were instructed "to settle all outstanding Bills and debts." 4

Early in March, 1871 the Caledonian described a musical convention held at McIndoe Falls, a sign of the current interest in music, and announced the spring-term opening of the Academy "with upwards of 60 scholars. Mr. Martin's increasing popularity proves him to be an efficient teacher and it is hoped that he will remain to keep the school in its present flourishing condition." The 

May 5th issue described the spring-term exhibition of Peacham Academy, consisting of orations, essays, declamations, and tableaux. We may safely assume that the closing exercises in vogue at McIndoes were similar. That month the 

Trustees engaged Mr. Martin for "the coming academical year as above, except for the plot of ground east of the Academy, which was voted to Mr. Norcross for the ensuing year," 5 as a garden or a pasture.

Early in June a strawberry festival was held at the Academy Hall, "the proceeds to be applied to repair the Church." 6 Shortly after the opening of the fall term, 378 residents of McIndoe Falls and Monroe attended the St. Johnsbury Fair via the Conn. and Pass. 

Rivers Railway. 7 Other events of the year were the Chicago Fire, the opening of the St. Johnsbury Athenaeum, the completion of 33

the western branch of the Portland-Ogdensburg Railway, and the destruction by fire of the "Lower" mill at Barnet. 8

The minutes of the January, 1872 meeting of the Association show that Oscar J. Gilchrist 9 and his cousin, W. H. Gilchrist, 10 were elected Trustees, Moses Bradford's name not appearing on the list of eight. Henry Gilchrist thus began a period of fine service to the Academy. In the spring, the Board re-engaged Thomas Martin and voted to pay him for the "Ensuing academical year" $150, of which $120 was the "whole income of the Academy funds." 11 

The very limited income from endowment at the disposition of the Trustees was then-as always—a harassing problem. In the early 70's, times were hard and even an old, established school like Phillips Academy (Andover) was having its severe financial difficulties. 12 The panic of 1872 was doubtless having its effect locally for, in 1872-73, Mr. Martin had only one assistant and the enrollment was 85, as compared to two assistants and 120 students the preceding year. 13

Shortly after the opening of the school on September 3rd, the Caledonian reported a meeting of the newly-formed McIndoes Falls Scientific Club. Several members presented and discussed exhibits: Dr. John M. Currier (algae; quartz),

A. P. Gilchrist (Indian relics), and Mr. Buchanan (corn fungus). Meetings were held at Dr. Currier's every fortnight. This group held a field trip in September, examining rock and lichen specimens between Harvey's and Sims' Ponds, returning to Deacon James Guthrie's home for supper, where the latter exhibited fossil and shell specimens gathered on his recent California trip. Several days later the group, led by State Geologist Cutting, examined rock specimens at a railway cut below Wells River. That week-end Dr. Cutting gave a series of three lectures entitled: "Metereology," "God in Nature," and "The Bible and Its 

Study." Rev. Mr. Norcross discussed "Motion" at a later meeting, held prior to the laying of the first rail on the Montpelier and Wells River Railroad. 14 

The scientific lectures had apparently caused some unfavorable comment, for the Caledonian of December 6th printed a letter from Dr. Norcross, defending 

Dr. Cutting and explaining that his presentation involved no conflict between religion and science.

Öther news events of the year, were these: a concert at St. Johnsbury by the 

Dartmouth Glee Club, a temperance lecture there by the famous orator, John B. 

Gough, the election of Grant, and the Boston Fire.

The annual meeting of the Trustees in 1873 resulted in the election of James Gilchrist as President, succeeding Rev. Mr. Norcross, John M. Child (s), Trustee, and three ministers from Barnet, Wells 

River, and West Barnet, Honorary Trustees. 15

The agitation over Dr. Cutting's lectures had not subsided, for he was unable to engage the Academy for a lecture subsequently given at Dr. Currier's home. 16 

That spring the Caledonian reported the building of a steam sawmill at Mount Tom, near Northampton, to which half of the sixteen million feet cut by the McIndoes Lumber Company were to be floated, the rest to be sawed locally. 

At two mid-summer meetings the Trustees planned to engage Principal Martin for another year, if money could be raised to shingle the building. The funds were not forthcoming: so they voted to "discontinue the school for the ensuing year (1873-74) and to devote the income of the fund to the necessary repair of the 

Academy building." 17 On August 22nd, the Caledonian announced that Martin had assumed the principalship of Bradford Academy and High School, saying: "The people of McIndoes regret very much to lose such an excellent teacher and superintendent of schools." In January, 1874, two new Trustees, John Matthews (Mathews) 18 and Rev. J. L. Litch 19 the new minister, were elected, the latter being chosen President, replacing James Gilchrist. 34

W. H. Gilchrist was elected Vice-President. It was voted to require "Unanimous consent" for renting the Hall, except for religious and scientific purposes." "20 That year the State Teachers' Convention held its 24th annual meeting at St. Johnsbury, which was also the scene of a lecture by Professor Young, Dartmouth scientist, and of entertainments by the Swiss Bell Ringers and Barnum's Circus. 21 In the spring the Congregational Society voted to buy as a parsonage the house then occupied by Eri Chamberlin, Rev. Mr. Litch having decided to remain as pastor. In August, the local dramatic society presented an entertainment at the Hall to reduce the debt on the parsonage. 22 At a December meeting the Trusteees engaged Jason Darwin Jenkins,23 A.B., as Preceptor for the term beginning January 5, 1875, and pay him $50 out of the fund, the latter to furnish wood and to have the tuition." 24 At the annual meeting in 1875 two new 

Trustees were elected, Harry Moore 25 and Alanson S. Moore. 26 

In the spring Austin H. Kenerson, 27 a senior at Dartmouth, was engaged as Principal for the fall term "for the sum of $50 for term and tuition and use of the building." 28 Like Henry Monteith, he was an alumnus of the Academy, having transferred from Peacham in order to be under Thomas Martin. 29 He had one assistant, the enrollment totaling 100 different students for the year. 30 At the conclusion of the fall term, Austin Kenerson returned to complete his college course and, after some indecision among the Trustees as to the feasibility of a winter term, was succeeded by a resident of Barnet, Robert J. Service, 31 who headed the Academy the winter term. 32 The Caledonian of December 31st described the 

Principal and his assistant, Charles Darling, 33 as "young men of culture and refinement, apt to teach." 

A new Trustee, Dr. Norcross, 34 was elected in January, 1876. 35 

The Caledonian reported on January 7th that "the finances of the corporation (Academy were never in so sound and healthful a condition as at the present time." Oddly enough, the same issue described the complete destruction by fire of the mill of the McIndoes Lumber Company, a great blow to the region. In February, the village ladies held a "Centennial Supper" and an entertainment at the Hall, featuring "Costumes of ye olden times,” a drama, and singing, to reduce the debt on the parsonage. 36 

At the end of the winter term, Robert Service returned to Dartmouth and was succeeded by Thomas Martin, who headed the school during the spring. 37 Thirty students were registered at the opening session. On February 25, the Caledonian also reported that R. E. Peabody had acquired an interest in the mill and was rebuilding; early in April the paper paid a well-deserved tribute to the late Ebenezer Brewer and his wife for their generosity to the

Academy and the Church. In August, the mill resumed operations under the new management and the Shephard Jubilee Singers gave a concert. 38

The Trustees engaged Austin H. Kenerson to resume his principalship at a salary of $50 per term with the usual perquisites. The fall term began September 1, 1876, with the Caledonian reporting 40 students in attendance. Later that month several prominent local couples, the Gilchrists, the Colbys, the Monteiths, the Wardens, and the Smiths left to attend the Centennial Exposition at Philadelphia. 

In November, the Reading Club gave an entertainment. 38 At their 

January meeting the Trustees elected a new Trustee, George H. 35

French, 40 and took action to secure a quit-claim deed to the land "deeded to the Academy by James and Walter Gilchrist." 41 

Early in February Caledonian subscribers read about the rumored departure of some fifty residents of the McIndoe Falls-Monroe area for the gold fields of the Black Hills. As one examines the records of that period, he is impressed by the large number of local residents who participated in the gold rush or who moved to the fertile lands of the Middle West. In fact, one could almost say that most of the pioneers in Green Mountain, Iowa were from Barnet.

The average attendance at the winter term was 20-25 students. 

The spring term opened on the 20th "with a fair attendance and with the same corps of teachers as heretofore.” 42 

The disputed election of President Hayes, a performance of "Uncle Tom's 

Cabin" at St. Johnsbury, Rev. Mr. Litch's trip to Boston to hear the celebrated evangelist, D. L. Moody, and the transfer-after some litigation of the Peabody lumber interests to Merrill & Van Dyke were the news stories of the winter and spring, as reported by the Caledonian. The financial affairs of R. E. Peabody had become seriously involved. Henry Merrill and George Van Dyke took over the operation of the business, the latter soon buying out his partner. 43 

Early in September, 1877, the Trustees engaged Henry R. 

Monteith, who had given up the practice of law, to serve again as 

Principal. Despite a premature announcement in the Caledonian that "the Academy would be closed for lack of patronage," the 

December 21st issue reported that “fall term under Mr. Monteith's direction" had been “a success in every sense of the word," and announced that 25 scholars were in attendance at the winter session. 

He was engaged to teach a full winter term and a half spring term for $75, plus perquisites. 44 Early in January, 1878, W. H. Gilchrist became President of the Board, replacing Rev. J. L. Litch, who had taken another pastorate. New Trustees elected were Isaac M. 

Smith, 45 John A. Gilchrist, 46 and Rev. N. F. Cobleigh, 47 the new minister. 48

The local correspondent of the Caledonian reported that month that the Van Dyke & Merrill mill was closed temporarily for lack of orders and that many were profiting by the abundance of snow and cold to coast on the Mill Hill. 

Late in the winter Dr. Brewer returned to visit old friends. The Hall was the scene of a play by the East Barnet Dramatic Club, an illustrated lecture by Rev. 

Mr. Cobleigh, and a sugar festival.49

In the spring of 1878 the Connecticut carried a log drive of forty million feet, part of which went as far south as Hartford, Conn. 

The big drive, a spectacular feature of life in the Upper Connecticut Valley until about 1915, and which had its large share of accidents and fatalities, was an economic boon to the region. 50 

The April 19th issue had this to say about the activities of the Academy: "Saturday evening last the Academy pupils gave an exhibition at the Academy Hall consisting of declamations, tableaux, and dialogues. The arrangements for the entertainment were made by Miss Cora Jennie and Miss Alice Camp, assisted by H. R. Monteith. The declamations and tableaux were well received and the dialogues elicited loud applause. It was conceded by all . . . that the pupils in their undertaking were eminently successful." A week later the newspaper reported that, at a meeting of the local school board district, it was voted to "instruct the prudential committee to make arrangements with the Trustees of the Academy to instruct a part or a whole of the pupils in the district in the branches taught in the common school."

The Academy was again under the leadership of H. R. Monteith in 1878-79. In November, the newspaper described the traditional public examination closing the fall term as being well received and later reported that some twenty pupils were in attendance at 36

the opening of the winter term. The Ladies' Aid Society sponsored a series of eight lectures that fall. In January, Rev. N. F. Cobleigh was elected President of the Board. 51 

The financial repercussions of the Peabody failure had been serious, one of the stores being obliged to close. The lumber company sold its Mount Tom property to the Connecticut River Lumber Company at Hartford. The drop in enrollment, as indicated above, doubtless reflected the poor local business conditions. In March, a course of lectures on Anatomy and Physiology was given by Dr. Bartlett. A month later J. B. Nelson of Monroe and Charles Moore, an alumnus and a son of Alanson, left for California, another sign of the difficult times. 52

At their August meeting the Trustees voted to re-engage Principal Monteith for two and a half terms of thirty weeks and to repair the building. In October, it was voted to release the Principal to permit him to accept a position in Unionville, Conn., and to engage Wendell P. Stafford, 53 a senior at St. Johnsbury Academy, to complete the term. 54 In a letter to the author, dated March 3, 1952, Mrs. Jennie 

Mulliken Sweet, 55 of Denver, a native of McIndoe Falls and once a district school teacher there, writes as follows: "I remember the house (Academy boarding house) as being the home of Henry Gilchrist and later, when I was in the Academy, age 13, it was the home of Prof. Monteith, and let me say that he was the best teacher I ever saw, here, or anywhere. Although he had been principal of a school in New York (where meantime he studied law and was admitted to the Bar), he couldn't help teaching; so he returned to the Academy to have a chance to teach his original methods... He wasn't there long, as the two said trustees (his father and his uncle)—although they gave him free rein in teaching—wouldn't buy him the equipment he wanted."

Writing to the author on August 28, 1952, Judge Stafford, then undoubtedly the oldest living principal of the Academy, sent these words of greeting: "In answer to your letter dated August 5th received today; with greetings to yourself and all old friends of McIndoes Academy. For the few weeks intervening between the departure of Principal Monteith and the installation of his successor, I, then a student at St. Johnsbury Academy, undertook the principalship of McIndoes Academy. As I am now in my 92nd year, I am hopeful that no one now living remembers how incompetent I was! "I remember the amusing and roguish Alex Nelson 56 who afterward became himself a lawyer and judge, as well as two rather mature young ladies 57 who knew much more Algebra than I did as to be embarrassing. "Your Uncle Alexander Dunnett! 58 How well I knew and how greatly I admired him! The leader of the Vermont Bar at the time of his death, one of the soundest lawyers I have ever known. Remember me to all who remember me."

At the end of the fall term Wendell Stafford returned to his studies at St. Johnsbury, and the Trustees engaged David J. Foster, 59 a senior at Dartmouth, as Principal for the winter term and, if profitable, for an abbreviated spring term. 60 The Academy is indeed fortunate to have had associated with it two young men, such as Stafford and Foster, who were to have so brilliant a future. 

Early in January William C. Gleason, 61 son of John C. and father of Horace W., was elected Trustee. 62 That year the Caledonian reported the construction of a sawmill and pulpmill at Dodge's or Canoe Falls (East Ryegate), the resumption of business at the McIndoes mill, and a county religious conference held in the village. In the summer the St. Johnsbury Orchestra gave a concert for the benefit of the Ladies' Aid Society and the village Republicans raised a Garfield banner and gathered to hear addresses. 63 37

CHAPTER XI THE ACADEMY IN THE 80's: ADMINISTRATION OF PROFESSOR LOCKE 

1 The Academy opened in the fall with George W. Bailey, A.B., as Principal. The Trustees were not able to grant him remuneration beyond the use of the Academy, since the income from the Fund was necessary for repairs, but did guarantee him "pay of the school equal to twenty scholars" and assured him winter and spring term employment on a contingent financial basis. 2 The school was again under his leadership in 1881-82. The Vermont Union of 

October 7th described feelingly the flurry of excitement in the village when Rev. Mr. Cobleigh's cows invaded the Academy yard. 

Apparently no bovine was encouraged—a student prank of the period to mount the terrifying academic heights to the cupola, there-like the papal mule-to survey the wonders below. In 

December the Caledonian reported that Henry Merrill had sold his interest in the lumber business to his partner, George Van Dyke, for $40,000 and also stated that there were 25 winter-term students at the Academy. 

At the annual meeting in 1882, Alexander Warden ³ was elected Trustee. President Cobleigh soon left for a new pastorate in the 

West, his successor being Rev. C. F. Morse, 4 formerly a missionary in Turkey. 5 This item appeared in May: "The spring term of McIndoe Falls Academy closed last Friday with prize speaking in the evening. The pupils acquitted themselves in a manner highly creditable to themselves and their teachers. Mr. G. W. Bailey concluded his second year as Principal. We regret that the Trustees are unable to continue his services longer."

It was voted to have a 32week school year in 1882-83 and Rev. 

Mr. Morse, an Honorary Trustee, announced that David B. Locke,6

A.B., had been engaged as Principal at "$125 for the year," with the use and care of the building. 7 Ône read this item in August: "We are happy to learn that D. B. Locke, a graduate of Dartmouth College and an experienced teacher, has been secured for our Academy for the ensuing -year," and on September 15th, "the fall term commenced last week, the attendance being some 40 scholars." In March 1853, the Trustees "voted 1. To assess a tax of 25 cents on the scholars attending the Academy, the money to be applied on repairs and incidental expenses of the Academy. 2. To forbid the playing of ball in the Academy yard except by permission of the Principal. 8 A February issue stated: "Professor Locke kept an excellent school for the winter term." In May one read that he was a "very popular teacher," and that the number of pupils had nearly doubled since he took charge. The spring log drive contained ninety million feet, of which twenty million remained in the booms for sawing at the local mill. 9

The contract issued to Mr. Locke for his second year provided for him to receive the income of the Fund ($150), plus two-thirds of the public money (ca. $40), after payment of .25 per scholar, or a total of $210.00. The 25 item was recommended by the local district committee as a tuition payment by district scholars attending the Academy, the sum to be taken from the public money received for said purpose. The Principal was assisted by Ellen L. 

Strobridge, Preceptress. 10 

The September 27th issue carried the news of the death of Deacon James Gilchrist, one of the founders of the Academy and a bulwark of the Board of Trustees, saying that the community was indebted 38

in a large degree to "his large-hearted generosity to the Church, the School, and all public works." 

In the fall of 1883, Barnet and Peacham nines played a game of baseball, Barnet winning, 26-25. The reporter commented wryly: "The Peacham boys play vigorously and swear profusely." 11 George Van Dyke had 150 men in his employ, and there was no empty tenement in the village. A month later a McIndoe Falls nine defeated Barnet by a margin of fifteen runs. Late in the fall $100 was spent on repairs at the Academy. Other events were a concert by the Colored Norfolk 

Jubilee Singers, a lecture by Rev. F. E. Healey of the North Monroe and McIndoes

Methodist Churches, and a promenade concert by the Barnet Band, all for the benefit of the Academy. 12 A November issue described the fall-term exhibition as follows: "The speakers deserve praise for their committing and successful delivery.” Thirty-seven students were enrolled at the beginning of the winter term. 13

Early in 1884 Alanson S. Moore was elected President of the 

Board. During the holidays the Academy students had presented 

Principal Locke with a hanging lamp and a cash gift. În March the Academy had 49 scholars. That spring the Van Dyke boarding house (the old hotel) was renovated, 14 and Rev. C. C. Cook 15 was ordained and installed as pastor of the Congregational Churches of McIndoe Falls and Monroe, replacing Rev. C. F. Morse. The former was the last pastor to serve both churches. About this time the 

Union Methodist-Universalist Church at McIndoe Falls was closed, being converted into a dwelling house. 16 One of the students at the 

Academy during D. B. Locke's administration, probably in 188384, was Oliver S. Warden ¹7 who became a national figure. 

The Trustees re-engaged David B. Locke as Principal for three terms. He was granted all the public money from the district, the money accruing from the individual tax, and the profit from the Hall. In September, the students were excused from school one day to attend the County Fair. That fall Grover Cleveland defeated 

Blaine for the Presidency and the first telephone poles appeared in the village. 18 At the annual meeting in 1885 Rev. Mr. Cook was elected Trustee. Later Principal Locke was engaged for his fourth year, his total salary for the year to be $200, including the profit from "too (sic) Entertainments and Exhibitions." 19

Early that fall one read that the Academy was "doing as good work as ever under efficient management" and that the mills were "busy day and night." 

Later Henry Ward Beecher lectured at St. Johnsbury and a music convention was held at McIndoe Falls. The public examination at the end of the fall term "showed thoroughness of drill." The winter term opened in December with 25 students. The McIndoes Choral Society met to discuss plans for a singing school and General Dow, the temperance orator, spoke at a local convention. 20 One of the active community organizations of the period was the McIndoe Falls Lyceum. Its members were: "Ora Bishop (President), Prof. Locke, Dr. Clark, Rev. C. C. Cook, C. L. Duncan, F. L. Clement, Dea. J. H. Guthrie, Carlos Hunt, and Newton Field." 21

At the annual meeting in 1886, W. R. Monteith was delegated to arrange with "this and other outlying school districts for a share of the public money by keeping a list of such scholars as may attend the Academy." Rev. C. C. Cook, the new President, was authorized to re-engage the Principal and to aid the latter in preparing a Catalogue. 22 The winter-term attendance at the Academy was the highest in three years, and the Academy "under the efficient management of Mr. Locke had never been more prosperous," "nearly fifty students" being enrolled for the spring term. 23 That spring the first telephone in the village was installed in George Van Dyke's office. In July, Martha J. Gleason ('59-'63) returned home from six years of missionary service in Turkey. 24

The Catalogue for 1885-86 25 appeared that summer. One of the two Honorary Trustees listed was George Van Dyke of North 39

Stratford, President of the Connecticut River Lumber Company which he, the principal stockholder, had organized in 1885 and which was then "the largest single lumber company doing business on the Connecticut." 26

Principal Locke was assisted by his wife, the Preceptress, and Dora F. Howe, 27 

Pupil Assistant, Helen Bruce 28 (Painting, Drawing), and Emma Dewey, 29 (Music). The enrollment during this year totaled 112 (fall-26, winter-35, spring-51), 60 different students (35 gentlemen, 25 ladies) being listed from eleven communities in four states (New York and Iowa, one each). The tuition ranged from $4.00 per term for the Common English Branches to $5.00 for the 

Classical Course, board ("There is no regular boarding house"), $2.25-$3.00 per week. The titles of the texts used do not show any marked change in general subject matter since 1853-54. Fewer texts were listed for Latin and Greek, however, reflecting increased emphasis on the non-classical course of study. The following statement is of interest: "There has been no graduating class for several years, but .. at least a dozen pupils . . . will be able to complete a course of study which will admit them to the Freshman Class in College on the certificate of the Principal or enter . . . business and social life with a good education. 

It is expected that a class will be formed at the beginning of the fall term, which will complete a course of study and receive a diploma from this institution. 

The course of study is calculated to fit students for entrance to any of our colleges, and . . . for the teacher's profession.” 30 ...

Following the departure of Rev. Mr. Cook, Alanson Moore again became President of the Board. 31 The fall term of 1886-87 opened auspiciously September 23 with "the usual number of students."

Business at the mill continued good, every tenement being filled. 

C. H. Hosford, 32 the last lawyer to practice in McIndoe Falls, formed a partnership with Alexander Dunnett of St. Johnsbury. 

St. Johnsbury celebrated its Centennial that autumn. Ďuring the school year the Hanoverian Family, the Guy Family, and the Bohemian Glass Blowers gave entertainments at the Hall, where

Miss Dewey's music class also presented a cantata, and Dr. 

Edward R. Clark, a series of lectures. The latter moved into the 

Chapel House, indicating the closing of the local Methodist Church. 

The Academy had about 40 students for the winter and spring terms, and "the debating society" was reported to be in "a very flourishing condition." The students presented a complete set of 

Dickens's works to Professor Locke. A promenade concert was given in March for the benefit of the school. On May 20th the Vermont

Union reported "a very successful term"; the rival Caledonian commenting that the "closing exhibition" had been "highly creditable." Offered a five-year contract, David Locke declined it, and ended his eminently successful career at the Academy that spring, going to Bradford to head the district school at a $1,000 salary. The 

30th issue praised him as "an efficient teacher, who will be greatly missed, under whose direction the school has advanced to a membership of forty.” 34

Events of the spring and summer were these: the departure of 75 men from the McIndoes mill, bound for Holyoke to replace strikers there; the arrival of the "largest drive ever" (June 23); Barnum's Circus at St. Johnsbury; a strawberry and ice-cream festival at the Academy; B. S. Simonds' 35 return from 

Dartmouth; July 4th fireworks in front of A. B. Perry's Store. 36 

In August, the Trustees engaged Walter M. Morgan, 37 A.B., as David Locke's successor, the Caledonian describing him as an "experienced and successful teacher." The Academy opened early in September. Three of its alumni left for college: Orren B. Somers (Norwich), B. S. Simonds, Jr., and Oliver S. Warden (Dartmouth). 41

When the winter term opened, there were "some 35 scholars, . with more to come.' "' 38

Mrs. E. R. Clark, Professor Morgan, Ben Simonds, and "Ollie" Warden. 41 The close of the winter term was marked by a promenade concert and supper. Early in March there was a heavy snowfall-two feet-a 30-inch fall being recorded father south and west. 42 In May, one read in the Caledonian that the floodswollen Connecticut had carried away McFarland's gristmill and the Mill Hill road to Monroe. The Memorial Day addresses were preached by Rev. Mr. Drew and Rev. Mr. Langford (Monroe). Later the comment was made that the latter's new church could be plainly seen from McIndoe Falls. In the June 21st issue the annual log drive was described as follows: "five miles of logs in the river, 600 men (400 below McIndoes), and 100 horses."

It was announced early in August that the Trustees had engaged W. R. Whitcomb of Springfield, a Dartmouth graduate (1888), to succeed Principal Morgan. The Records show that his classmate, Wallace Clark Short, 43 A.B., became Principal in 1888-89, his compensation being $100, plus the usual perquisites. Another newcomer in the village that fall was Dr. J. M. Gibson, 44 who was to be the local doctor for some forty years. In November, Mary 

Howe, Brattleboro, the Vermont soprano and "American Patti", gave a concert at the county seat. The fall and early winter were marked by the election of Harrison, the installation of electric lights at St. Johnsbury, and the purchase of its first snowroller by that town. 45

At the annual meeting in 1889 the Association elected the following officers: A. S. Moore, President; I. M. Smith, Vice-President; 

W. R. Monteith, Secretary; W. H. Gilchrist, Treasurer, and A. B. 

Perry,46 a new Trustee, Auditor.47 Several school benefits were held that academic year in the form of promenade concerts and oyster or chicken pie suppers, the goal being a $200 repair fund, of which $125 was spent for new seats. In March, the Vermont Union announced that the railroad (renamed the Passumpsic Division of the Boston & Maine) had adopted coal fuel, thus eliminating the large wood business done at Barnet and at McIndoes to supply the wood-burning locomotives. A log drive of eighty million feet, a series of gospel meetings, and the visit of Governor Dillingham featured the spring and summer. 48

In August, the Trustees announced that B. F. Adams, 49 A.B., "highly recommended as a gentleman and a scholar," had been engaged as Principal, his salary being $125, plus the regular perquisites. 50 That fall three suppers and promenade-concerts, one by the South Ryegate Cornet Band, were given for the benefit of the 

Academy repair fund. Baseball uniforms arrived for the local boys who, thus inspired, defeated Peacham 4-0 in five innings. The local 

Knights of Pythias dedicated their new hall over Bishop's Store.

Winch, which caused great depredations among the squirrels and other small fauna, was a source of much local excitement. 51 The

The lumber mill resumed operations with a force of eighty men and a stock of twenty million feet. 54 Following the departure of Principal Chapin, after only three weeks, because of meager returns from tuition and a low salary, Editor Chase of the Union urged the incorporation of the Academy and the district school, as at Lyndon. 55 43

CHAPTER XII THE ACADEMY IN THE 90's 

The Trustees were not able to maintain a school in 1890-91, and the people of the village were roundly critized by Editor Chase for allowing the Academy to close. In November, a concert was held to pay for the new street lights. 1 Meeting on January 12, 1891, the 

Trustees "voted to have a school and to engage Thomas Martin if possible, and to paint and repair the Academy building." 2 On 

June 18th one read that the log drive had passed McIndoe Falls, leaving twenty-five million feet to be cut at the mill, then employing 100 men. Late in August President Harrison visited St. Johnsbury, his train stopping at McIndoes. He shook hands with little 

Bessie Hunt, who is one of the few alumni ever to shake hands with a President, at least in McIndoes. 3 

The Trustees were fortunate in securing again the services of Thomas Martin, a scholarly, experienced teacher well acquainted with the Academy and the region. The fall term opened with 30 students. There was a larger enrollment in the winter term. One read about the destruction by fire of the Laughlin Block and Brock's Hotel at Barnet and the dedication of the Fairbanks Museum. The winter concert-lecture series was opened by Mr. 

Cooper of Boston who entertained with a phonograph. The closing exhibition of the winter term was arranged as a benefit, the program comprising a drama, short dialogues, speaking, and singing. (Admission: 15c, 25c). 5 In March, Rev. William M. Gay 6 came as the new minister. The McIndoes Lyceum held a debate early in 

July on the subject: "Resolved, that the World's Fair should be closed on the Sabbath." 7

Disaster struck the village again in August, 1892, when the boxshop burned, with a total loss, including several million feet of lumber, of $50-$60,000. The mill, built in 1876, was one of the six or seven operated on the Connecticut by the Company. 8 

The Academy opened that fall with the "small attendance of 15 scholars," more being expected shortly. A fall event was the opening of the winter Lyceum season with a debate on this question: "Resolved, that the lynch law is sometimes justifiable." B. Riley, M. Turner, W. Pearl upheld the affirmative; W. Glenn, A. Guthrie, and F. Chase, the negative. Peacham defeated McIndoes in baseball, 18-9. Many local residents attended a husking bee at the 

Liddle Brothers' farm. 9 The Academy closed its fall term in mid- 

November with a promenade social soon after the election of Cleveland. Thirty "scholars" were in attendance for the next term. 

A series of gospel meetings and a children's Christmas-tree party at the Academy closed the year. 10 In the winter the departure of 

Dr. George A. Dickson ('85-'86) for the West, the staging of an operetta by Miss Dewey's girls, a play by the "Academy scholars," were local items. That spring the name of the local post office was changed to McIndoe Falls, the "s" having been dropped, and Wen dell P. Stafford delivered the Memorial Day Address. In the sum mer occurred the visit of ex-Governor Pingree, a basket-picnic at Harvey's Lake and the coaching parade in the White Mountains. 11 44

The termination of Thomas Martin's regime marked-with two exceptions—the end of the long line of Dartmouth-trained principals. Thereafter, graduates of Middlebury and Vermont predominated. 

In August, the Trustees engaged Rev. D. C. Townsend 12 of 

Grafton as Principal for 1893-94, and the latter planned to "offer a regular graded course for college preparation." Only 18 students were present when the fall term opened on September 7th. Rev. 

Mr. Gay presented a lecture on the Chicago World's Fair for the benefit of the Academy. One fall social event was a promenade arranged for his pupils by Principal Townsend. The winter term announcement advertised "the special attention to the commercial branches," to aid the many one-term students from the farms. An old-fashioned spelling school and oyster supper climaxed a series of student-sponsored entertainments which raised enough money to paper and whitewash the South Room. The students tendered the Principal a surprise party in late December. 13 Early in January, 1894, Wendell P. Stafford gave a lecture on Whittier at the 

K. of P. Hall. Other winter and spring social events were a promenade sponsored by the Young Ladies' Society, a concert by the St. 

Johnsbury Academy Glee Club, a promenade concert sponsored by the G.A.R., and sugaring-offs at the sugar houses of Alanson Moore and Philo Van Dyke (hayrack transportation provided). An 11-10 basketball game at the St. Johnsbury Opera House was a novelty. 

The Academy closed late in April after a "very successful term," 

Rev. Mr. Townsend leaving for a position at Pella College (Iowa).

Memorial Day was well celebrated, an address being given at Monroe by ex-Governor Farnham. A banquet at the Hall, with music by the Barnet Band, was one of the attractions. Independence Day was marked by fireworks furnished by P. B. Van Dyke. 14 

In July, the Association met to elect Horace W. Gleason 15 and 

Charles L. Duncan 16 as Trustees to replace Isaac M. Smith and 

William C. Gleason, recently deceased. At the suggestion of Thomas 

Martin, steps were taken to determine who held stock in the 

Association, the latter, W. H. Gilchrist, and G. F. Winch serving on a committee for that purpose. 17 It is said that the Academy was on the verge of being closed in 1894 and that G. F. Winch and others helped to keep it open. The following month Don C. 

Andrus, A.B.,18 was engaged as Principal. "Mr. Andrus was assisted by Fred S. Wright, A.B., 19 and Miss L. S. Andrus, Preceptress. A promenade concert netted $15 for shingling the Academy. The school opened early in September with 35 students, the largest number in considerable time." (Peacham had about 50 students the same term). In October, Thomas Martin played in the band at 

Lyndon Fair. That month two promenade concerts and oyster suppers were held, the second raising $24 for a reading room. The last half of the fall term opened with "42 scholars." The closing exercises are described below:

Although the Academy was in operation between 1896 and the late fall of 1904, the author has not discovered any records of the Association between the former date and 1907 when the Academy was reopened after a three-year lapse. Fortunately, much information for that period in the form of one catalogue (1898-99), numerous programs, newspaper references, and two diaries is available.

L. Perry 25 and by Mrs. Anna S. Frost (Elocution, Physical Culture).

Van Dyke Block (opposite Winch's Store). The Catalogue for 1895-96 lists three classes: Middle (1), Junior (13), Sub- 

Junior (17), in addition to the Unclassified (28). There were three courses of study: Classical (1), Latin-English (18), English (12), Unclassified (28). The attendance summary for 1894-95 is: fall, 42; winter, 52; spring, 40; that for 

1895-96 is: fall, 46; winter, 54. The following excerpts are of interest: "McIndoes

Academy offers three courses of study of four years each, arranged especially to fit pupils for college, for teaching, and for the active duties of life. Á regular diploma will be presented to each pupil who satisfactorily completes any one of three courses. The Classical Course is intended primarily for those wishing to pursue the corresponding course, leading to the degree of A.B., in any college. This course comprises twelve terms of Latin, nine of Greek, four of History, four of Literature, three of Algebra, three of Geometry, two of Phonics, two of English, Grammar, and Analysis, and one term each of Arithmetic. Letter-Writing, Civil Government, Physiology, Rhetoric, Chemistry, Psychology, Anglo-Saxon, and Political Economy. The Latin-English Course is planned for those desiring to continue in any college a similar advanced course leading to the degree of Ph. B. for teachers, and for all (especially ladies) whose school life will end on leaving the Academy. The only difference between this and the Classical Course is, that in place of Greek, are substituted the following: 

English Composition, Physical Geography, Botany, Astronomy, French or German 

(four terms), and Geology. The English Course aims to give a good general understanding of all the leading Common and Higher English branches. It fits the pupil for a scientific course in college, for teaching, or for business life. It differs from the preceding course in substituting for Latin the following: 

Conant's Vermont, Commercial Geography, Drawing, Commercial Arithmetic, English History, French History, Bookkeeping, Business Law, Physics, Zoology, and Trigonometry." 

Comments in the historical sketch are worth citing: "Since August, 1894, about two hundred dollars have been expended on the building and the grounds. 

A few thousand dollars added to the present endowment would enable the institution to double its present sphere of usefulness." Among the former 

Principals listed is Charles Adams, concerning whom the author has no information. It cites among the alumni, in addition to ex-Governor Pingree, Hon. 

Theophilus Grout, Editor of the Exchange and Standard, and President Gates of Iowa College. "Much attention is now being paid to this important branch of study (Elocution). Provision has just been made for this department of the school. A series of lessons will be given at any time, whenever ten or more applicants so desire. In connection with the regular work of vocal training, lessons in Physical Culture will also be given without additional expense. 

Mrs. Frost has appeared several times in the Academy Hall before large and appreciative audiences. . . . Lessons will also be given any term by a professional penman on the application of twenty-five or more students. The tuition charges per term are listed as: "Common English, $5.00, Higher English, $6.00, Languages, each, extra, $1.00, Penmanship (12 lessons), $1.50, Elocution (10 hour lessons) $5.00, Instrumental Music (20 lessons) $8.00." Great stress was laid on elocution (Delsarte System) and on penmanship (Spencerian System) in the schools of the 80's and 90's. (Mark Sullivan, Our Times, Vol. II).

In the winter term a school magazine, the McIndoes Academy Messenger, 42 appeared, its aim being "to arouse the students to write original themes, to plan stories, and to compose poems." It contained poems by Flora Winch and Lillian Bishop, conundrums by Mabel A. Ford, and articles and essays by E. E. W., Grace Page, and Grace Crane. A feature editorial eloquently urged the Town of Barnet to support the Academy. One article described the reorganization of the McIndoes Academy Debating Society, whose membership was enlarged to include "lady students." Forty-three names were added to the roll, making a total of fifty-seven members. The officers of the Society were: President Charles L. 

Gleason, Vice-President, Albert W. Gibson, Secretary, Grace A.

Page, Treasurer, William A. Wright. Later in the winter the Union spoke in complimetary terms of Principal Webster's work and announced that “about fifty scholars" were enrolled for the Spring term. Another Messenger article described the operations of the 

Commercial Department, newly installed in the North Room. The 

Ellis Tablet Method of Bookkeeping, the Munson Shorthand Sys tem, and the Zanerian Penmanship System were used, and a new Smith Premier typewriter was in operation. The total enrollment reached 55 that winter, 62 being in attendance during the two terms, drawn from eight communities in the two states. An effort was made in town meeting that March to have the "town make the Academy a central high school and to pay the tuition for those who have reached that grade." This action was urged by Editor 

Chase of the Union as a means of keeping tuition money in town and also because of the difficulty of maintaining high standards in the upper grades of the district schools. Although the town did not act favorably on this proposal, it was soon to assume its growing educational responsibility by paying the tuition of its students of high-school age. 43

CHAPTER XIII EARLY YEARS OF THE 20th CENTURY 

During the summer months of 1900, the McIndoes Band was active, giving an open-air concert on the Fourth and playing for two promenades. This was the era when the derby hat and the shirt waist were indispensable items for the well-dressed man and woman. The buggy was the stylish method of local transportation, but it was soon to be a dangerous one-particularly on the treacherous Barnet Mountain Road-whenever its skittish motive power encountered a wheezing chariot of new design. 

Fortunately, Principal Howe and Nora Lockwood returned that fall. The enrollment was 49, about evenly divided between boys and girls. 2 The American Literary Society sponsored three debates on its Lyceum program, the subjects being the relative merits of 

Alexander the Great and Hannibal (the latter won), political parties (McKinley defeated Bryan in November), and capital punishment. 3 In September, the Class of 1901 held a promenade social (essays, recitations, and songs; McIndoes Orchestra), to pay its "graduating expenses" and another benefit on December 28th ("gentlemen 25c, ladies free"), refreshments, 20c per couple. 4 The

Class of 1902 held a promenade and old-fashioned supper in January, also staging a farce, "How Katie Proposed." The admission was based on height (gentlemen, three cents a foot, ladies, two), the proceeds being used to purchase dishes for "future Academy entertainments." The newspaper reported a Lyceum debate and a supper promenade, the latter organized by the Village Improvement Society. In February, 1901, the Seniors presented a benefit program of reading, recitations, and music, followed by the farce, "Gumtown Women's Association," with this cast: Mary Frazer, Myrtie Skinner, Margaret Gleason, Grace Skinner, Emma Crane, Lottie Roy, Lloyd Murray, and John Aiken. Shortly after the opening of the spring term, the Alumni Association staged "Down by the Sea." Members of the cast were: C. D. Howe, Frank Willey, John Roy, Dudley Fitzgerald, John Gilchrist, Fred Hastie, Lillian 

Bishop, Mabel Ford, and Genevieve Stevenson. Young's Orchestra played for the promenade. 5

On August 22, 1904, the year that the Panama Canal was started, Monroe celebrated its Semi-Centennial with a picnic and a pro gram of songs and speeches at Dickinson's Grove. 25 The Academy 56

opened for the fall term of 1904-05 with Henry F. Harvey, 26 A.B., as Principal, apparently unassisted. Events of the month were the raising of a Republican flag for Roosevelt and Fairbanks, and the shutting down of the lumber mill, all the available logs having been sawed. Lillian Bishop, Daisy McFarland, Alice Moore, and 

Myrtie Skinner were teaching at Peacham, West Danville, and

Concord that year. A reception was tendered Representative-elect 

Walter Phelps, whose sons Harvey, Leonard, and Perley, like their sister, attended the Academy in the late 90's. Harvey and Leonard visited the St. Louis World's Fair that fall. 27 The village had a distinguished visitor in October when Governor C. J. Bell of 

Walden was the guest of his brother-in-law, A. B. Perry. În November, the Seniors held a promenade and masquerade. That month the Union announced that the Academy would close at the end of the fall term, not to reopen for the present, and that Mr. Harvey would return to Bristol, 28 

Undoubtedly the principal reason for closing the Academy was the slump in enrollment and the consequent shrinkage of the income from tuition. This situation, combined with the minor financial panic of 1903-04, the uncertainty and decline of the lumber industry, and the lack of endowment, proved to be more intolerable than those exigencies which the school and its backers had patiently endured on previous occasions. The destiny of an institution or of a community is often subject to individual caprice. 

It will be recalled that members of the Fairbanks family formerly resided in Barnet. Having experimented successfully with a new type of scale, they sought to obtain water-power rights on the Stevens River for its manufacture. Unable to do so, they moved to 

St. Johnsbury, which soon became an important manufacturing and cultural center. 29

It is a local tradition that McIndoes Academy lost a golden opportunity to establish itself on a firm financial basis when, at the turn of the century, it was on the point of receiving a handsome bequest of $75,000. The prospective donor is said to have been dissuaded by a local citizen whom he had consulted. As a result, a 

New England college was his beneficiary. 

The deadline of the local lumber industry soon reached its ultimate conclusion, for the big lumber mill was dismantled prior to the tragic death of George Van Dyke in 1909, its machinery being shifted to Mount Tom to replace that of a mill destroyed by fire. 

The lumber yard and the abandoned buildings became the informal playground of the village youngsters and the victims of the ravages of time. The village elders, wistfully reminiscent of the glories of the past, spoke hopefully of the industrial opportunities presented by the unharnessed Connecticut. 30 

The Records throw light on an interesting phase of the early corporate life of the institution; viz., the holding of stock in the 

Academy Corporation or Association. It was formerly necessary for a Trustee to be a stockholder. There is a record of the transfer on July 12, 1894, of one share of stock from Luther S. Parker of 

Peacham, one of the first subscribers, to Thomas Martin of Barnet, the transaction being witnessed by John Varnum, Jr. and F. H. 

Varnum. On the death of Charlotte Buffum Martin, it was trans- 57

ferred by her executor, Elijah W. Lyford, to Dr. J. M. Gibson who, in his turn, transferred it February 21, 1907, to Milo A. Gibson, who was soon to become an Honorary Trustee. Leonard A. Phelps has a stock assignment (2 shares), originally owned by Simeon Heath and transferred in 1894 to Thomas Martin, then to Alexander Warden the same year, and finally to Walter M. Phelps in 

1911. 31

The old-fashioned promenade, which was one of the mainstays of school and village social life, and which is now apparently completely superseded by the round and square dances, flourished at the Academy until the 1920's, at least, when round dancing-previously frowned upon-was introduced at the Hall. The author recalls with nostalgia the stacking of the folding chairs after a play. This noisy preliminary ceremony over, it was the convention for the gentlemen to seat themselves on the old-fashioned benches which lined the west wall of the Hall, the ladies occupying the corresponding position on the east side. Brazen or brave, indeed, was the maid or man who dared to reverse this arrangement. When the orchestra or pianist (often Inez Edgerton) gave the signal, the more courageous males walked-sometimes dashed or slid-across the floor to invite the chosen one to promenade. It was considered an honor and an action involving both courage and savoir-faire for a couple to lead the promenade. The first two brave souls took their stand at the north end of the Hall, leaning more or less nonchalantly against the stage. Gradually other couples followed suit. When the double line was deemed sufficiently long, the music started in earnest and the head couple walked sedately or skipped down the center, parting company at the foot in order to retrace their steps singly along the sides. When the first couple met again at the head of the floor, they performed some of the rudimentary steps characteristic of the square dances, such as "grand right and left," the other couples following suit. An effort was made to introduce complicated maneuvers and when, on occasion, a head couple "reversed the field," there was much excitement. Occasionally a "ladies' choice" number was called, thus increasing the romantic excitement involved in the choice of a partner. Essentially a simple form of dance, the promenade, which could be both graceful and vigorous, was a wholesome form of entertainment. 

During the three years when the Academy was closed, the local students were forced to go away to school. The Hall continued to be the social center of the village, the Grange presenting its play, "Among the Breakers." In this period the local graded school teachers were Mr. and Mrs. M. C. Rodgers. The appointment of 

Mrs. George F. Winch as Postmistress (1905), the renovation of the parsonage, the building of a long side-track by the Boston & Maine, a stereopticon lecture by Rev. Guy Roberts of Monroe, a visit from 

Dr. and Mrs. Leonard Dickson of Montana, the meetings of the 

Book Club, the defeat of Company D by Brattleboro (21-20) for the state basketball championship, the Glidden Tours (automobile) in the White Mountains, and the appointment of Henry C. Ide as 

Governor-General of the Philippines were events of the period. 32 58

CHAPTER XIV REOPENING OF THE ACADEMY 

The year 1907 was eventful locally, for it saw the reopening of the Academy, and nationally, for it witnessed the Jamestown Exposition, Marconi's trans-Atlantic wireless conversation, and a business depression and financial panic. ¹ In January, the Company 

D basketball team defeated Dartmouth 25-23 and the local Book

Club held its annual meeting. A local entertainment (supper, farce, minstrel show, and promenade), the sale of the Perry House to 

R. M. Johnson, Bryan's speech at St. Johnsbury, and the selection of Harvey Burbank, Principal of Phillips Academy, Danville, as 

Superintendent of the Barnet, Danville, Walden, and Waterford

Union District ($1250 salary) were other happenings. The river road below McIndoe Falls was washed out that spring, teams being obliged to use the stage-coach road (now paved) over Smith's Hill. 

W. M. Phelps went to Iowa for a carload of horses; Rev. Mr.

Eldridge accepted a position in the same state; W. H. Gilchrist drove to Boston in his new touring car; and the West Barnet Grange presented a play at the Hall. 2 In August, 1907, the local baseball team defeated Lyndonville at Monroe, 10-4. A benefit cotillion party for the team was held on the Academy lawn, with a phonograph furnishing music. 3

The "stockholders of the McIndoes Falls Academy Association" met at the Academy, August 24th "for the purpose of filling vacancies in the offices of the Association and to see what arrangements can be made with the Town School Directors as to maintaining a school the coming year in the Academy building." President W. H. Gilchrist being absent, Vice-President C. L. Duncan presided. Denzil M. Crane 4 and Dr. John M. Gibson 5 were elected 

Trustees to replace C. I. Smith, deceased, and A. B. Perry. H. W.

Gleason, Clerk, was delegated to supervise repairs and to obtain a copy of the charter. Meeting on September 9th, the Trustees discussed the finances of the Association and voted to have a school the winter term. Messrs. Duncan, Gibson, and Gilchrist were instructed to secure a teacher and D. M. Crane, to canvass the enrollment situation. At later meetings, Dr. Gibson reported on negotiations with the candidates. 6 Among the alumnae teaching that fall were these: Emma Kent, Myrtie Skinner, Alice and

Lena Moore, and Nellie Manchester, long a teacher at the local primary school. Mrs. J. C. Gleason celebrated her 96th birthday.

Other fall events were these: the organization of a girls' basketball team at St. Johnsbury Academy, a dance at Van Ďyke Hall, a lecture course at Barnet, a harvest dinner at the vestry, a husking bee at the Crane Brothers' farm, and the hiring of Toby Matthews, the highest-salaried player in New England, by Company D. 7 

The date, December 2, 1907, was a banner one for the Academy, for it reopened after a three-year lapse, with Leslie E. Collins 8 as 

Principal. The Trustees concluded an arrangement with the latter, guaranteeing him “23 scholars for winter and spring terms, in addition to $50 from the fund per term." He boarded at Henry Bell's home. The Academy was in session both terms. In the early spring, however, Principal Collins resigned because of ill health, being re- 59

placed by Edward E. Bruce 9 of Boston. Shortly after the turn of the century, Vermont towns were required by statute to provide for the secondary education of their children in local schools or elsewhere. The Barnet Town Report (1906) mentions the payment of tuition for pupils going out of town. The arrival of Rev. Henry T. 

Barnard, a concert by the Lotus Quartette, the dedication of Brightlook Hospital, a box-supper social, and an entertainment by Fred French, netting $35 for the Academy piano fund, were winter events. 10

The first reference to a local basketball game is in the Caledonian of February 5, 1908, Monroe defeating Barnet at the Academy 

24-18. That season the Company D team won 37 out of 43 games.

An heroic game was played at the Hall in late winter when the 

McIndoe Falls "Ancients" defeated the Barnet "Roughs" 8-7. Such stalwarts as H. F. Chamberlin, C. L. Duncan, J. M. Gibson, D. H. 

Gilchrist, Clarence Hilliard, R. S. Kelley, R. M. Johnson, and

G. F. Winch took the floor against Moses Bruso, John Wallace, et al. The Academy girls organized a basketball team that winter, playing three games with a local young ladies' team (2-8, 6-4, 4-19).¹¹

The spring was featured by the arrival of a carload of automobiles for J. H. Stuart, the opening of the boarding house for the rivermen, G. F. Winch's candidacy for sheriff, and pre-Memorial Day talk by James Guthrie at the graded school. 12 The Academy closed early in June, the final program comprising a play, "The Maidens All Forlorn," staged by the Swastika Club, a farce given by the students, and a reading by Professor Bruce. Three weeks later Taft and Sherman were nominated. 13

In mid-July, the Caledonian reported the burning of the creamery and a meeting of the Academy Alumni and Trustees held to interest the public in the establishment of a high school in McIndoe 

Falls. The Alumni Association elected the following officers: Presi dent, Milo A. Gibson, Vice-President, Flora M. Winch, Secretary, Emma B. Kent, and Treasurer, D. Roger Crane. At a public meeting, much interest was shown in the reopened school. 14 În mid-August, the Trustees elected Milo A. Gibson 15 an honorary member. The latter reported that Millard C. Moore 16 had been appointed Principal, with Grace Fuller, 17 A.B., Assistant.

On August 19, 1908, the first Old Home Day celebration organized by the Alumni Association was held at the Academy. Field and track events, a tug-of-war, two baseball games, a program of speeches, recitals, and a promenade were scheduled. In the senior baseball game, McIndoes defeated East Ryegate 10-9 in ten innings with Van Dyke and Frazer as the battery. Among the speakers introduced by W. H. Gilchrist were Professor Bridgman, Samuel B. 

Page, former student and assistant (1856), Professor Bunker, Lucy 

C. Richardson, Superintendent Burbank, Rev. Mr. Barnard, George

L. Hunt, Milo A. Gibson, and H. E. Barnet. The evening program included numbers by Mrs. Edgerton and Misses Winch, Moore, Clement, Hall, Scott, and Mrs. Morgan. Brock's Orchestra played for the promenade, which netted $50. Among the alumni present were several who had attended the first term of the Academy in 1853, notably John S. T. Wallace, Lucy C. Richardson, Jane Sutherland, Mary Duncan, Mary C. Trask, and Jane Peabody. Another former teacher, Nora Lockwood, was also present. 18 60

Late in August, the Trustees voted "to expend a sum not to exceed fifty dollars ($50)" for a physical laboratory. The fall term opened September 1st, shortly after a Republican rally at St. Johnsbury, addressed by Senator Borah. A reception was held for Professor Moore and Miss Fuller the following week. 19 That fall occurred these events: Dr. Grenfell's speech at St. Johnsbury, Taft's victory over Bryan, the opening of a branch library (Addie M. Carr, Librarian), a student-sponsored benefit entertainment, the election of Robert W. Simonds as State's Attorney, and the presentation of the plays, “Black Beauty," "Uncle Tom's Cabin," and "Beverly of 

Graustark" at St. Johnsbury, 20 On December 12, 1908, the Trustees rescinded the action of November 20th against the use of the Hall for basketball and voted unanimously for such use from Jan. 1, 1909 for the rest of the school year, providing it involved no expenses for the Trustees, under the following restrictions: "Rule I. No one not a registered student of the Academy (excepting the opposing team in a regularly-scheduled game) shall be allowed to play or practice basketball. Rule II. Non-students are not to be allowed in the Hall during practice. Rule III. Any schedule of games made must be submitted to the Principal and majority of the Trustees for approval. Rule IV. Any student who falls below 70 in his scholarship for any one week shall be debarred from playing Basketball during the following week. D. M. Crane was appointed to see that the windows are properly guarded and that the Hall is in proper shape for Basket Ball." 21

In January, a stereopticon lecture on the Panama Canal was held at the Hall, as were two basketball games, the Academy team losing to the Monroc Warriors 7-33 and defeating Peacham 21-4. 22

The members of the 1909 girls' basketball team were Bertha Bedell, Ethel Duncan, Florence Gibson, Hazel Warden and Marion Morgan. 

23 There were two courses of study in 1908-09: the Latin and the Modern Language, which included Greek, German, Declama tion, Commercial Arithmetic, Commercial Law, and Bookkeeping, in addition to English, History, Mathematics, and Science. Tuition was "eight dollars per term." This statement is of special interest: "Town pupils who have passed the required examination will have the tuition paid by the town." There were opportunities for selfboarding. 34 In May, the Trustees engaged the same teachers for 

1909-10, and the students staged a comedy, "Old Acre Folk.” 25 

The Catalogue for 1909-10 carried this information: "McIndoes Academy is an approved school of the first class under the laws of the State of Vermont. It has also been approved by the Superintendent of 

Education of the State of New Hampshire for students from the town of Monroe, N. H. Graduates of this institution are admitted without examination "to 

Colby, Middlebury, and Norwich. . . . Pupils from both Barnet and Monroe, after passing the required examination, are entitled to have their tuition paid by their respective towns." The Academy opened on August 31st. The enrollment totaled 28 in three classes. 26

The meeting of the Association was held in January, 1910, at Dr. 

Gibson's home. The author recalls such meetings; and, although the concise reports of the Clerk do not reflect the constant struggle to maintain and improve the school in the face of many obstacles, the competition of other schools, and some opposition, he was often aware of the concern and sacrifice of those supporting it. 27

Academy. 27

A.B., as Principal and Elizabeth L. Olson, 33 A.B., as Assistant, and an attendance of over 40 students. The Seniors and the Alumni gave the customary reception. The Commencement Exercises in 1911, the first since the reopening of the school, began on May 28th with the Baccalaureate Sermon by Rev. Mr. Hawk. The Alumni Association met late in June to plan for the fourth annual Old Home Day and to arrange for the repainting (white, the original color) of the Academy. The celebration was held late in August and was attended by "hundreds of former students and friends." Remarks were made by Superintendent Burbank and telegrams of good wishes were read. The Association re-elected its officers and voted its thanks to the Hon. Gilbert Hunt 38 for his devotion and help to the Academy. The traditional ball game was terminated prematurely by rain, but the evening festivities-reading, solos, and promenade-were not dampened. 39 

The Academy was fortunate in having the services of Mr. Cameron and Miss Olson for 1911-12. The tuition had been increased to ten dollars per term; the attendance was forty-five. The statement, "Many of the students board themselves, thereby lessening the expense," shows that the days of self-boarding were not yet over. 40
