This summer marked the end of a relatively peaceful decade in American history, for the war clouds in Europe were soon to spread their shadows and release their pent-up storm over much of the world. But there was a hope of better days, too, for the 

Report carried this statement: "The village is full of surveyors this week planning for the development of its water power." Sixteen years later the hope was realized in that impressive hydroelectric station, the Comerford Dam, and subsequently by the McIndoes Plant, and the large project currently being undertaken by the New England Electric Sys., a development amply justifying the faith of the older generation in the Connecticut. 66

CHAPTER XV THE WORLD WAR I PERIOD 

The Academy opened in 1914-15 shortly after the outbreak of 

World War I, with Carl E. Howland, ¹ Á.B., Principal. He had two assistants, Katherine Graves, 2 B.S., and Helen E. Humiston, 3

A.B. The enrollment was higher-46—than in recent years. The tuition increase raised the rate to forty dollars. This was the last year that the so-called "tuition examinations" were required of candidates for high school admission. 4 The Old Home Day stage success, "The Country Minister," was repeated at St. Johnsbury 

Center shortly before "The Shepherd of the Hills" and "Way Down

East" appeared at the Colonial Theatre. Other events that fall were the meeting of the State Teachers' Association at St. Johnsbury, Vice-President Marshall's visit to that town, an Anti-Saloon League lecture, a stock company play and vaudeville at the Hall, a student picnic on Hunt's Mountain, a Senior masquerade-promenade, and the victories of Dillingham, Dale, and Gates in the State election. The British marching song, "Tipperary," 5 was then much in vogue. The annual meeting of the Association was held at the home of C. L. Duncan in 1915. The Academy basketball team defeated Peacham 21-13 and Newbury 32-10, the players being H. 

Smith, O. Quimby, N. Van Dyke, S. Darling, W. Chamberlin, N. 

Chamberlin, and P. Emery. The Seniors presented a comedy, "Quits," the cast including Hazel Beattie, Gladys McDonald, Mary 

Wells, Perley Emery, and Mr. Howland. 6

The Class of 1915 was graduated June 4th, a month after the sinking of the "Lusitania," the graduates being: Hazel A. Beattie, ("Evolution of the West"), Gladys M. Clark ("Would Washington 

Care for Us Now?"), George Samuel Darling ("America and Her 

President"), Hazel V. Kay (History; Prophecy), and Mary E. Wells ("Early Days in Barnet"). The invocation and the address were given by Rev. D. E. Burns and Rev. Donald Frazer, respectively. 7

The acting President of the Alumni Association, Jean D. Phelps, reported on August 16th a $75 payment for teachers' salaries, a substantial payment for new chairs, and an Old Home Day profit of $168. She also spoke of the endowment campaign for $1000, expressed appreciation of the skilled counsel of Rev. C. B. Bliss, 8 and praised Peacham Academy for raising $6,000 within a fivemile radius. 9 The eighth annual Old Home Day was held on 

August 24th. The principal speaker was Rev. S. G. Barnes, with reminiscences by W. A. McLam of Concord, N. H., student at the 

Academy fifty years before. The Association elected Mrs. Jean D. 

Phelps, President, and Florence I. Gibson, Vice-President. 10

The new Principal in 1915-16 was George H. Gardner, 11 A.B., with Miss Graves as Assistant. The Juniors held the traditional September reception for the teachers and the Freshmen. 12 The winter was marked by a Christmas tree party, a K. of P. entertainment at the Hall (Ryegate Male Quartet), a play, "The Teaser" (Gladys 

McDonald, Mr. Gardner, Miss Graves, Harold Somers, Inez Fisher, Alex Gibson, and Ray Hilliard) at the Barnet Town Hall on January 28th, a basketball game with Peacham, and a box-supper prom- 67

enade held by the Alumni Association to celebrate the successful $1000 endowment campaign. 13 The Baccalaureate Sermon was preached on May 28th by Rev. Mr. Bliss, the address to the Seniors being given by L. B. Richards. The graduates were Percy G. Kendall ("The Deadwood Mail"), Gladys MacDonald (“American Relief 

Work in Belgium"), and Owen H. Quimby ("The U. S. Navy"). ¹4

The ninth annual Old Home Day was celebrated on August 24th. 

After a dinner served in the vestry, the 400-500 guests adjourned to the Academy campus for speaking, with Rev. Bliss serving as toastmaster. The main address was given by Rev. F. H. Laird, with Rev. D. E. Burns, Rev. W. C. Clark, and Milo A. Gibson also on the program. Folk dancing by the children, baseball games, a minstrel show by the Monroe Grange, and the drama, "Willow Dale," were other features of the day. 15 

Although the war had been raging for two years, it had not yet engulfed the United States. But the sinking of the "Sussex" was an evil omen. The raid of General Villa led to the American punitive expedition under General Pershing. That was the era of military preparedness sentiment, the Plattsburg Camp, war gardens, and Liberty Bonds. 16 

The Faculty for 1916-17 consisted of Mr. Gardner and his new 

Assistant, Rita L. Forbes, 17 A.B., with an enrollment of some 30 students. 18. The war became increasingly absorbing and menacing. 

The narrow victory of Wilson over Hughes and the perils of submarine warfare captured the headlines and crowded the personal items in the Caledonian, although there were a play and the traditional Christmas party at the Hall. 19 At the January, 1917 meeting of the Association, D. M. Crane was elected Vice-President, replacing C. L. Duncan (deceased). 20 The Academy had a flagraising ceremony on April 5, the day before the declaration of war on Germany. The Trustees voted to engage Mr. Gardner for the ensuing year-a contract later voided by the latter's entry into military service—and to increase the tuition to $45 a year. 21

The Commencement Week program began with_the_Baccalaure ate Sermon by Rev. F. S. Tolman, followed by graduation on June 

15th, a few days after registration for the first American draft since the Civil War and the formation of the Vermont Home Guard. 

The graduates were these: Inez S. Fisher ("New Hampshire Heroes of the Revolution"), Raymond C. Hilliard ("Vermont"), Laura V. 

Bort (History; Prophecy), and Julia C. Smith (“Our Flag"). Orchestral and choral numbers and an address by Rev. G. A. Martin completed the program. 22 

That fall Harold E. Young, 23 A.B., and Mrs. Lillias M. Warren, 24 A.B., were the teachers. The war had its serious impact on the Academy, for both the Principal and his Assistant left for war service, being replaced by Julius V. Sturtevant, 25 A.B., and George

E. Davies, 26 It was an era of grim headlines, of "Hooverizing" (food conservation), and of heatless and wheatless days. The village dedicated its flag on September 2nd. 27 At the 1918 meeting of the

Association, the Trustees elected C. Brock Duncan to fill the vacancy left by his father's death. It was the coldest winter in fortyfour years, with 74 zero (or below) days. There was a Red Cross benefit in February, and the Seniors gave a play, "No Trespassing," 68

on March 1st. That spring the school and the community were saddened by the death of Sergeant Samuel Darling, killed in action at Seicheprey on April 20th. 28

Kendall, R. Darling, R. Parker, Ô. Ward.

Basketball, as played during the first quarter-century after its invention in the 90's, was a rugged and rough sport, particularly in the smaller cities and towns where courts were small and frequent physical contact was inevitable. As an additional risk, courts were often studded with pillars, overhung with low girders, or lined with projecting benches for the spectators. Occasionally a red-hot stove was on the playing surface. Floors were often slippery with dance-floor wax or were made of loose-fitting, worn pine boards which, filled with dust and splinters, were a hazard for the players. 

As a result, knee and elbow pads were commonly worn. Ignorance of the rules was widespread and officiating was lax, fouls being seldom called except for slugging or tripping. Ground rules often permitted a player to spring to a wainscoting for an unobstructed step shot or to use a ricochet pass off the wall. The low-scoring, close-contact game of those days seems far removed from the highscoring, comparatively open game of today.

CHAPTER XVI THE 1920's

During the summer two modest structures of antique functional design which, half hidden under wild grapevines, had long stood under the graceful elms behind the Academy, succumbed to the march of progress and were replaced by the latest word in sanitary plumbing. 11

The school was fortunate in having the services of Mr. Pitkin and Miss Thompson again in 1922-23. The enrollment was about 

35 and the tuition had been increased to $75 per year. Three courses of study, approved by the State Department of Education, Latin, Sub-Latin, and English, were offered. Two tennis courts were constructed that summer. 12 The Spitfire, successor to the

In November, there was a chicken pie supper at the vestry, followed by a Hallowe'en party at the Hall. The sum of $40 was spent for science equipment. Á later issue spoke of the indebtedness of the Academy and urged more support for the institution. Several of the boys attended the Older Boys' Conference at Newport. The May number announced the gift of some books by Mr. Rogers. 13 

A disastrous fire laid waste the business center of the village on February 14th, 1923. Starting in the Grange Hall on a bitterly cold night, fanned by a high wind, the fire destroyed some twelve buildings, including Winch's Store, Guthrie's Drug Store, and the old Perry Store on the west side of the square, and the large Van Dyke Block, the "Company" Store, and several other buildings on the east side. The $60,000 loss was a cruel blow to the village. 14

Chamberlin (Salutatorian; "Interesting Evidence of Evolution") and John F. Whitehill (Valedictorian; "Glimpses of the Immigration Question", U.V.M. Schol.) Fifteen Mile Falls Lower Development of the New England Power 

Co. The work started in late August, 1928 when large crews of workers and engineers (a total of 2500 men) moved into the area, many to stay for several years, some to become permanent residents. 

This population influx was an economic asset to the region, and the hydro-electric development was to bring immeasurable benefits.

CHAPTER XVII THE 1930's 

The Academy opened on September 2, 1930, with Mr. Ellsworth continuing as Principal, assisted by Miss Munn, Mabel Severance, ¹

B.S., and Bernice G. Sargent, 2 A.B. Fifty-one students were enrolled. 3 Four weeks later an event of major importance to New 

England occurred in Washington when President Hoover pressed a button which officially opened the Comerford Station, the largest hydro-electric plant in New England, located at the foot of the Fifteen Mile Falls, some six miles from the Academy. In addition to providing electricity for consumption in northern New England, the huge plant sends power to Massachusetts on a 125-mile 

220,000 volt transmission line. An engineering and scenic marvel, the Lower Development is a mecca for tourists, an industrial boon to New England, and an important source of revenue for Barnet and Monroe. The population of Barnet rose from 1685 in 1920 to its peak of 2604 in 1930. Monroe's population increased from 

356 in 1920 to 457 in 1930. 4

Late in December, Dr. Gibson, President of the Board, who had gone to Kentucky for his health, died in Berea, thus ending a life-time of unstinting service devoted to the Barnet-Monroe- Ryegate area and to the Academy. At the annual meeting in 1931, held at the home of D. M. Crane, the latter again became President and Treasurer, H. F. Chamberlin, Vice-President, with H. W.

Gleason continuing as Clerk. 5 The Spitfire, directed by Laura Walker, reported an American History class trip to Ticonderoga and an illustrated lecture on Labrador. The boys' basketball team (K. 

Gates, Captain) won nine games, losing seven. The girls' team (L. 

Walker, Captain) avenged earlier losses by defeating Newbury. 6

"Progress of Forestry in New England"; Prophecy; U.V.M. Schol.).7

The major local event of 1931 was the opening, in March, of the McIndoes Station of the Connecticut River Power Co., (subsidiary of the New England Power Co.). Located on the site of the old dam and employing 11 men, it provides power for northern 

New England and for Massachusetts. Like Comerford Station, it is a great asset to the region. 

The new Principal in 1931-32 was George R. Tyler, 9 A.B. Mrs.

Bailey, Miss Sargent, and Mrs. Ellis Crossley, 10 A.B., were Assistants. Sixty-five students were enrolled. That year saw the reestablishment of a Commercial Course with Mrs. Crossley giving instruction in Typewriting and Shorthand. Some ten students were taking music (band) lessons from George McLure. Page. The basketball team (R. Duncan, Captain) was very success- 77

ful, winning 18 out of 20 games. The baseball team, also captained by Duncan, did well, defeating Wells River, Groton, and St. Johnsbury (8-0), losing to Peacham 5-4). A school band was organized.

In the fall of 1936, the Academy was headed by Chandler B. 

Mosher, 30 B.S., with Mrs. Bailey, Paul S. Winch, 31 A.B., and Miss

Walker as Assistants. In the Presidential election, Roosevelt defeated Landon, only Maine and Vermont remaining in the Republican column. Among the events recorded by the Spitfire (Gladys 

Page, Editor) were these: a debate with St. Johnsbury Academy, an operetta, "Hearts and Blossoms," and a visit to the Ryegate Paper

Co. mill. The girls' basketball team (G. Page, Captain) had a good record, winning 10 out of 15 games. The boys' team (R. Carlisle, Captain) did even better, winning 15 out of 19 games. A Senior party at the Monroe Town Hall on April 16th was the first Academy social function to be held there. The Senior Play, "Streamlined 

Sue," was presented early in May. The baseball captain was Francis

Gochee. At the annual meeting of the Academy Association in 

March, Wendell P. Roy was elected President, G. L. Frazer, Vice- 

President, H. W. Gleason, Clerk and Treasurer. Later the Trustees voted to increase the Principal's salary to $1800. 32

The faculty for 1937-38 was unchanged in personnel. The tuition remained at $75 a year. Principal Mosher was instructed by the 

Trustees to ascertain the cost of introducing a course in Home Economics. No further action was taken in this matter. The Spitfire (Pauline Emery, Editor) chronicled the Senior Play, "Trapped," and the presentation of a state tournament consolation plaque to the basketball team (R. Carlisle, Captain). Lyle Lang was chosen for the all-tournament team. Avis Merrill captained the girls team.

A student orchestra was a great boon to the school this year. The yearbook described the renovation of the Academy, new hardwood floors in the South Room and in the Hall, new desks, and concrete walks and landscaping having been provided. 34

The year 1938 witnessed Hitler's seizure of Austria, Howard 

Hughes' flight around the world in less than four days and the

Nazi invasion of Czecho-Slovakia. War seemed imminent when the 

Academy opened on September 6, 1938, but appeasement was the watchword at Munich and Prime Minister Chamberlain returned to England promising "peace in our time." Mr. Mosher was assisted by Mrs. Bailey, Miss Walker, and Mary I. Cook 36 (Music). The Spitfire was headed by Irene Quimby. School events recorded in the Easter issue were the December concert by the combined Glee Clubs and Orchestra, a box supper and dance, talks on vocations, a lecture on Tennessee missions by Rev. Mr. Woodworth, a Senior minstrel show, and the operetta, "Sunbonnet Girl." The basketball team was captained by Karl Rutledge. The girls' team (I. Quimby, Captain) had a fine season, winning all 10 games. 37 The guest speakers at Commencement were Rev. A. V. Woodworth and Rev. Thomas R. Burns. The sixteen graduates were these: Richard H. Amidon (History), Edythe M. Bogie (Prophecy),

The Academy students returned to their classes September 3, 1939, two days after Hitler's armies rolled across the border of Poland and started World War II. The faculty list was unchanged.

CHAPTER XVIII WORLD WAR II AND THE POST-WAR YEARS 

As the danger of war increased in September, 1940, the House passed the Selective Service Bill. The Academy reopened, with Mr. 

Mosher again in charge and 57 students enrolled. Catherine Shalucha, B.S., had succeeded Miss Cook. 2 The Spitfire, headed by 

Jean Woodworth, mentioned the Senior Play, "Personality Plus,' presented soon after Roosevelt's victory over Wilkie. A Glee Club cantata, a dancing class, the Vermont Music Festical, an operetta, "Words and Music," and the Senior trip to Boston were highlights of the year. The girls' basketball team (Amidon and Tyler, Captains) had a good record. The boys' team (G. Carlisle, Captain), lost to Chelsea, winner of the Class C Tournament at Waterbury. 

That spring the Trustees elected George L. Frazer, Sr., President and Wendell P. Roy, Vice-President. The 1940 Census gave Barnet a population of 1596, Monroe, 430. 3

The Baccalaureate and the Commencement Exercises were held at the Church in June, with Rev. J. R. Treganza and Rev. J. H.

Sargent as speakers. The graduates were these: Marian L. Amidon, Joyce E. Bailey (Valedictorian; "The School and the Community"; 

U.V.M. Schol.; Prophecy), George D. Carlisle (President; Balfour Prize), Richard M. Carter (History), Elmer W. Chamberlin, Elwyn M. Gibson (U.V.M. Schol.), Dorothy E. Johnson, Frederick W. 

Knights, Pauline A. Lang (History), Elizabeth A. Peters (Prophecy), 

Paul A. White (Prophecy), C. Jean Woodworth (Salutatorian); "Democracy in Our School"). 4

Severe air raids on London, the meeting of Hitler and Mussolini at Brenner Pass, and the German invasion of Russia marked the spring and summer months. 5 Locally, the Ladies' Aid Society sponsored a carnival and "Ye Old Tyme Singing School," and the Barnet Center Church celebrated its Sesquicentennial. 6 

In 1941-42, the only faculty change was the replacement of Miss 

Shalucha by Mrs. Marguerite Mariotti. 7 The enrollment was sixty five. The loss of two American destroyers and the invasion of Indo- 

China stirred the people, and the news, received on a quiet Sunday in December, that Japanese planes had bombed Pearl Harbor, shocked the nation into action. The Spitfire (Rachel Moore, Editor) reported the Senior Play, “Bringing Home the Bacon," and the successes of the touch football team (J. Sund, Captain) and the basketball teams, (D. Choate and M. Tyler, Captains). An operetta, "Chonita," a sugar party, and a Pan-American promenade were chronicled by the Caledonian-Record. 9 

Shortly before Commencement, General Wainright abandoned Corregidor, and the U. S. Fleet won the crucial Battle of Midway.10 

The principal addresses were delivered by Rev. A. V. Woodworth and Rev. T. M. Taylor. The members of the graduating class were these: Minnie J. Balch (Prophecy), Calvin S. Bogie, Caroline M. 

Cady, David W. Choate, Marjorie L. Farnsworth, Doris I. Hunt, Elmer S. Johnson, Erlene M. Locke, Alice E. Moore, Rachel E. 

Moore (Salutatorian; "Patriotism and Its Development"; History), 83

(President; Balfour Prize; U.V.M. Schol.), Adria A. Worster. 11 In

Treasurer. Steps were taken to continue the annual renovation of the building and to reinvest a small fund in a Government Bond. 12 

In the fall of 1942, two months before the Allied invasion of 

North Africa, Osman P. Hatch 13 succeeded Mr. Mosher as Principal and Rev. Reginald Nichols 14 took over Mrs. Mariotti's duties. The tuition was increased to $100 per pupil. As a result of wartime restrictions, sports were curtailed this year and the next. 15 The Cale donian-Record reported a Glee Club pageant, "Miracle of the 

Clock," on Dec. 7th, and operetta, "The Gypsy Rover," on April 

29th and 30th. The Spitfire (Jean Bailey, Editor) was dedicated to the alumni in military service. It chronicled the Senior Play, "High, Wyde, and Handsome," and an "Old Plantation Party.” 16

Shortly after Commencement the Allies invaded Sicily. Two months later the British troops landed in Italy. In 1943-44, Mr.

President, Treasurer Gleason was instructed to invest the Walter Gleason Fund of $2,000 and the Scott Farnum Fund of $2,000, a recent gift, in War Bonds. 27

The small wartime Class of 1944 attended its Baccalaureate service on June 4th, the day the Allies captured Rome. The speaker was Rev. C. C. Salisbury. The Seniors were taking their final examination as the Allied troops under General Eisenhower invaded 

Normandy. The Commencement Address, "Graduating into a

The Board of Trustees, as constituted in the 100th Anniversary 

Year, comprised the following: Albert D. Gilmour, President; 

Robert L. Kendall, Vice-President; Lyle M. Frazer, Secretary, Wesley L. Moore, Treasurer; and D. Roger Crane, Horace W. Gleason (Trustee for sixty years), Herman T. Griffin, Gladys B. Roy, Wendall P. Roy, Linwood F. Whitehill. The present Honorary Trustees are George H. Amidon, Milo A. Gibson, and George L.

Frazer, Sr. Recently the Trustees have done much to improve the buildings and grounds, providing modern lighting, a bell system, and insulation, redecorating certain rooms, and grading the parking-playground area. 

Commencement Week had unique significance for the Class of 1953, which was graduated as the Academy completed its century of existence. The Baccalaureate Sermon was preached at the 

Church on June 14th by Rev. Ernest Thomas, the Class Day program followed on the 15th. The Commencement Exercises, preceded by a banquet at the McIndoes Inn, were held June 16th at the Monroe Town Hall. The Class and the Academy were honored by the presence of Senator and Mrs. Ralph E. Flanders of 

Vermont. A native of Barnet and a great-great-grandson of Simeon 

Heath, one of the original Subscribers, the Senator, as guest speaker, reminisced about the early history of Barnet and of the Academy and spoke convincingly about the dangers of demagoguery, stressing the importance of maintaining human values in building a better world. 78

The members of the graduating class were: Leonard E. Aiken, Mary J. Annis, Patricia E. Bedell, Pauline A. Blanchard (Men's Club Schol.), Ruth V. Buskey (History), Everett L. Chase, Ruth 

E. Cheney, Ronald W. Crowe (President; Danforth Award), Joan

E. Crown (Balfour Prize; Vt. Junior College Schol.; Men's Club Schol.; Danforth Award), Charles L. Goss (Salutatorian; U.V.M. 

Schol.), Reginald D. Hatley, Avis L. Paradie (History), Edith I. 

Paye, Carol A. Sheltra (Valedictorian; U.V.M. Schol.), Richard W. 

Strew. 79

In terminating this chronicle of the Academy, a few words concerning the school and its present status will be pertinent. Still governed by its self-perpetuating Board of Trustees, the Academy, which is one of the twenty-three approved private Academies in the State, is certified and inspected by the New Hampshire and 

Vermont Departments of Education. The local Superintendents are: 

Llewellyn Roberts (Barnet) and Phil A. Bennett (Monroe). The minimum secondary school tuition payment which the State of Vermont requires each town to pay per pupil has recently been increased from $175 to $225. The Towns of Barnet and Monroe have for several years each generously appropriated a sum above the legal minimum, the current grants being $7,500 and $8,000, respectively. The present enrollment of the Academy is 69 and is drawn from five towns (Barnet, Bath, Monroe, Ryegate, and Waterford) in two states. Three courses of study are offered: College 

Preparatory, Commercial, and General. The traditional foreign languages, French and Latin, the Sciences, English, History, and Mathematics are taught. The Commercial Department, recently expanded, now possesses eleven typewriters and other modern office equipment. 80 90

Throughout the years the Academy has often been aided by the fund-raising projects of interested friends, students, and alumni. 

The recent book drive conducted by the students, together with donations and funds from a one-act play contest provided some five hundred volumes as a nucleus for the expanding library, now in new quarters. The recently reorganized Alumni Association, whose officers are Larkin H. Burrill, President, Carlisle Griffin, Vice-President, Theresa K. Pratt, Secretary, and Harold Kimball, Treasurer, has raised funds for the repainting of the Academy building and has been actively engaged in planning the celebration of the 100th Anniversary of the school. The Centennial Committee has as its 

Co-Chairmen, Albert D. Gilmour, President of the Board of Trustees, and Dorothy N. Somerville, representing the Alumni. Other members of the Committee are: Marian A. Kendall, Secretary-Treasurer, Robert L. Kendall, Publicity, Agnes H. Little, Pageant, Patricia L. McLure, Music, and Lyle M. Frazer, Program Chairman.

This anniversary is one which the Academy shares with the New York Central and with the petroleum industry. It is celebrated some fifty years after the founding of the Barnet Library, one year after the 75th Anniversary of the East Barnet Chapel, and one year before the 100th Anniversary of the present Barnet Church edifice and that of the town of Monroe. 81 

So, as the Alumni and friends of McIndoes Academy gather on August 22, 1953 to celebrate the Centennial of the founding of the school, their reunion on its campus will bear testimony, as in the days of their parents and grandparents, to the satisfaction and strength to be found in voluntary association in a common and noble cause, a principle dear to the American way of life. 91

CONCLUSION 

In tracing the history of McIndoes Academy, we have tried to consider the many and varied factors influencing its founding and its development. Every school models itself more or less after the institutions of its type, yet no two are alike. Each possesses, to a greater or lesser degree, a pattern of its own,-one resulting from the environment and the individuals shaping its growth. 

Located in a quiet village in a beautiful valley, it is conceivable that, with certain favorable developments, such as a large endowment or reliance on a more stable industry than the lumber business, the Academy could have maintained its large and promising early enrollment drawn from a wide area. In fact, given the stabilizing and constructive influence of an able educator over a long period, it might even have attained considerable prestige in New England and elsewhere, like Deerfield Academy which, a struggling village school when Dr. Boyden became its Principal some fifty years ago, has grown into an institution of national reputation under his guidance.

But fate decided otherwise. The ups and downs of the local lumber business, its ultimate collapse, and the lack of bequests made the establishment of a suitable endowment impossible. As a result, the Academy was soon engaged in a battle for survival. A substantial migration from Vermont, the shift of the population from rural to industrial areas, and the development of more rapid means of travel-all combined to curtail the enrollment. Because of these negative factors and a lack of funds, the Trustees found it impossible to retain some of their more able Principals and Assistants. 

This rapid turnover in personnel was, obviously, a handicap. 

The Trustees, however, maintained their self-sacrificing efforts and stubborn loyalty to a worthy cause, the education of the young people of the region. As a result, despite its manifold difficulties and obstacles, the Academy has been closed only three times for a total of five or six years in the century of its existence. It has seen the development of the high school, the disappearance of many contemporary academies, and the conversion of others into public or semi-public secondary schools. It has witnessed the passing of the ornamental subjects from the curriculum, the shift of emphasis from the classics to the modern languages, and the rise of the commercial and vocational subjects. During its lifetime, the rural district schools, once so numerous, have dwindled in number and, in some towns, have completely disappeared. 

Some of the Principals of the Academy have served Barnet as Superintendent of Schools. Early in the present century that office was abolished locally and a District Superintendent took over the supervision of the schools in several towns. Another development in this field has been the closer liaison between the town schools and the State Department of Education, resulting in better supervision and improved standards, accompanied by a stronger financial backing of both primary and secondary schools. 

During the past forty-five years, and more particularly during the past two decades, the Academy has seen a rapid growth of in- 92

terscholastic sports, a wider participation in extra-curricular activities, and the formation of an interscholastic association fostering both sports and non-athletic activities, such as dramatics, music, and public speaking, with their rich possibilities for cultural growth. 

The Academy is not, and never has been, essentially a collegepreparatory school. A minority of its graduates have gone on to college. The majority of its alumni-probably about two thousand, past and present-have gone immediately into business, farming, government service, home-making, nursing, and teaching. (Over 

1600 names are on the incomplete list prepared while this book was being written.) Some of the earlier alumni have been cited for their achievements in education, government, journalism, law, and medicine. Many of more recent vintage have achieved distinction in such fields as agriculture, business, education, local and state administration, medicine, military service, nursing, public relations, and social work. 

Easily accessible for those students of the neighboring communities who, because of geographic and economic factors, would find it difficult, even impossible, to go elsewhere to secondary school, the Academy provides the stimulation, training, and vision necessary for college work or for assuming the immediate responsibility of earning a living or making a home. It is increasingly able, thanks to its enlarged facilities, to give a basic education to its young people and to train them morally and socially so that they will be eager to develop and use their native ability, be proud of their heritage of sturdy independence and local government, and will have a real appreciation of the responsibilities of citizenship and of their roles as members of the community. 

Thanks largely to the extensive hydro-electric development on the Connecticut, the past quarter-century has produced greater financial stability for the local communities and a strengthening of their economic fibers, with the result that they are better able to meet the challenge and the responsibility of making adequate provision for the education of their children. This amelioration has been evident in the physical improvement of the Academy and in the betterment of its facilities for serving the region. 

It is apparent, as we pay tribute to the founders of the Academy and to its friends and supporters throughout the century, that their faith, hard work, and sacrifice have been justified, and that this institution with a larger and more stable enrollment during recent years is in a relatively stronger position than for some time and is better equipped to meet the educational needs of boys and girls from the farms and villages of New Hampshire and Vermont. 

As McIndoes Academy enters its second century of service, it should continue to enjoy, as in the past, the loyal support of all those who believe in the great contribution to be made by an institution which is close and dear to the people it serves a contribution to self-reliant citizenship and to human values. 93

Wells, p. 167. 26-Born at Charlestown, Mass., Thomas Martin (1846-1899) was brought up in Peacham, where he prepared for college. Member of the class of 1871 at Dartmouth (A.B., A.M.), he earned his way through school and college by teaching. After graduation he continued his teaching at McIndoes Academy, leaving in 1873 to become Principal of Bradford Academy and H. S. After a period of ill-health, he served briefly as Principal at McIndoes in 1876, going on to principalships at Lyndon Institute, Newport H. S., and Barton, H. S. Forced again by illness to give up his successful career, he returned to McIndoe Falls, serving two years more as Principal in the early 90's. He married Charlotte Hunt Buffum of McIndoe Falls and lived in the Benj. Manchester House. A lover of music, as well as a scholar and excellent teacher, he also served as Supt. of Schools in Barnet and Peacham, Dartmouth Alumni Magazine, Nov. 17, 1899; Wells, p. 596. 27-Records, 1,40,41,118. 28-The former (1851-1912) was born in Topsham. Eminent minister and educator, he served as president of three colleges: Iowa College (1887-1900), Pomona College (1901-1909), and 

Fisk University (1909-1912). He was awarded an honorary D.D. (Andover) and an honorary 

LL. D. (Nebraska); General Catalogue of Dartmouth College, 1910-11 (Concord: Rumford).

Graduated from Dartmouth in 1882, he served McIndoes Academy as Principal for five years (1883-87). Later he was Principal of Bradford Academy and 

Supt. of Schools in Wichendon, Mass. and Rutland, Vt. A sound educator, he established courses in Domestic Science and in Art and originated summer-school teacher-training courses. Retiring to the old homestead in Corinth, he farmed, traveled extensively, and served as Supt. of Schools, Representative, and Chairman of the State Teachers' Retirement 

Board. His first wife was Nellie V. Winch, sister of George F. Winch, county sheriff, postmaster, and a merchant in McIndoe Falls. Loan of Howard Smith. It belonged to his mother, Mary L. Paddleford, who with her cousin Ellen, was a student at the Academy under David B. Locke. 26-Born in 

Stanbridge, P. Q., George Van Dyke (1846-1909), a commanding and energetic man, gave his life to the lumber business. He came to McIndoe Falls in 1877 and became the head, not only of the local mills but also of very extensive timber and lumber interests in Maine, N. H. and Vt. One of his associates called him "the most forceful and picturesque man in the lumber business in the U. S." He met his death in a tragic accident at Turner's Falls, while following the drive. A niece attended the Academy in 1885-86 and several other children of his brother Philo, his local business representative and a prominent citizen, were students there in later years. Wells, pp. 214-215, 650-651; Contact, Vol. 11, No. 4, 1-3.

1-A native of Haverhill, C. E. Howland was graduated from Dartmouth in 1914. After leaving McIA, he was in business and in teaching prior to serving in the Navy. (1903-13) and at McIndoe Falls (1914-1923). Scholarly and an enthusiastic worker with young people, he played a major role in the preparation and publication of the History of Barnet. APPENDIX 

CHARTER OF McINDOES FALLS ACADEMY 

The Acts and Resolves passed by the General Assembly of the State of 

Vermont at the October Session 1853. 

P. 81.

No. 81, An Act to Incorporate the McIndoes Falls Academy. 

It is hereby enacted by the General Assembly of the State of Vermont as follows: 

Sec. 1 James Gilchrist, John C. Gleason, Walter Gilchrist, William A.

Weeks, Azro Dutton, Samuel F. Gleason, Charles Peabody, Luther F. Parker, 

Simeon Heath, James McArthur, John M. Child, William R. Monteith and

Horace Duncan, their associates and successors, are constituted a body politic and corporate with all the rights, powers, duties, and privileges incident to similar corporations, for the purpose of maintaining a literary and scientific institution to be known and called by the name of McIndoes Falls Academy. 

Sec. 2 Said corporation may have a common seal, and the same alter at pleasure; may sue and be sued by their corporate name; make their own bylaws, rules and regulations not repugnant to the constitution and Laws of the State; may take and hold by gift, grant, devise, purchase or otherwise, real and personal estate to the amount of Fifteen Thousand Dollars, and the same manage and dispose of for the benefit of said corporation. 

Sec. 3 The persons named in the first section of this act are hereby constituted a board of Trustees for the government and management of the interests and concerns of said Academy, and may hold and discharge the duties of said trustees until others shall be appointed trustees, agreeably to such bylaws, rules and regulations as shall be adopted for that purpose. 

Sec. 4-A meeting of said corporation shall be holden at the Academy at said McIndoes Falls, previous to the first day of January, A. D. 1854, for the purpose of adopting by-laws, rules and regulations for the purpose of carrying out the purposes of said corporation; And said meeting may be called by any three of the said Trustees, and notice given to the others, either in person, or by sending written notice, at least four days before the time appointed; and a majority of said trustees shall constitute a quorum for the transaction of business. 

Sec. 5 This Act shall be under the control of future Legislatures, to alter, amend or repeal, as the public good may require. 

Approved December 1, 1953. 

Barnet Town Clerk's Office, November 12, A.D. 1948, at 10 o'clock and 0 min.

A.M. received the instrument of which the foregoing is a true copy. 

Attest James J. Ritchie,

Town Clerk A True Copy-
